2026年 10月 30日 全5頁

10月会合で日銀は政策金利を据え置き 
2％の物価安定に向け利上げ継続へ 
利上げの悪影響は今のところ限定的、12月に1.5％へ追加利上げか

経済調査部 チーフエコノミスト 神田 慶司 
  エコノミスト 秋元 虹輝

[要約] 
◼ 日本銀行（日銀）は2026年10月29、30日の金融政策決定会合で、短期金利を1.25％
程度に据え置いた。日銀の政策運営は、基調的な物価上昇率を「2％に近づける」局面
から「2％程度で安定させる」局面に移った。今後は、経済・物価・金融情勢に応じて
政策金利を引き上げ、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく方針だ。 
◼ 日銀は2024年 3月以降、金融政策の正常化を進めてきたが、企業・家計への悪影響は
これまでのところ限定的だ。企業では比較的良好な資金調達環境やネット有利子負債
比率の低下が、家計では収入の増加や借入返済額の減少に加え、住宅ローン金利上昇
の返済額への反映の遅れが、足元の負担増を抑えている。ただし、家計の借入残高は
収入対比で高水準にあり、金利高が長期化すれば、返済負担が徐々に高まる可能性が
ある。 
◼ 当社では、日銀は 2026年 12月会合で短期金利を1.5％、2027年 3月会合で 1.75％へ
引き上げた後、いったん据え置くとみている。ただし、その後も金融環境がなお緩和
的で物価上振れリスクが残れば、同年6月会合で2.0％への追加利上げに踏み切る可能
性がある。

2026年 10月会合で日銀は政策金利の据え置きを決定 
日本銀行（日銀）は2026年10月29、30日に開催された金融政策決定会合で、無担保コール
レート・オーバーナイト物（以下、短期金利）を 1.25％程度に据え置くことを決めた1。今回
は利上げを決定した翌月の会合ということもあり、これまでの利上げの影響を見極めることを
重視したとみられる。

1 日本銀行「当面の金融政策運営について」（2026年10月 30日）

### Page 2

1

2 / 5

連続利上げに慎重な姿勢は、前回会合後の総裁発言からもうかがえる。9月会合直後の記者
会見で植田和男総裁は、「大きな幅の利上げとか何回も連続して利上げするというようなこと
は、端的に申し上げれば、物価が目標を超えてものすごい上がっていくというリスクが高いか、
あるいは既に目標を超えちゃっているかというようなことに依存するんだと思います」2と述べ、
その例として2022、23年の米欧の高インフレ局面を挙げた。 
また、日銀が保有する上場投資信託（ETF）については、2026年 1月以降の売却が市場に大
きな影響を与えることなく円滑に進捗したことなどを踏まえ、2027年 1月から年間の売却ペー
スを簿価で 3,300億円程度から 8,600億円程度（市場全体の売買代金に占める年間売却額（時
価）の割合は0.05％程度から0.1％程度）に引き上げることを決めた3。

インフレ基調は2％目標と概ね整合的で、今後は定着に向けて利上げ継続へ 
9月会合の声明文で「2％に近づいている」4と評価した消費者物価の基調的な上昇率につい
ては、10月会合で公表された「経済・物価情勢の展望」（展望レポート）で「2％の『物価安定
の目標』と概ね整合的な水準」に修正された。さらに、「従来は、基調的な物価上昇率を 2％に
向けて引き上げていく過程にあり、物価の下振れリスクに相応の注意を払うことが必要であっ
たが、足もとでは局面が変化しており、今後は、基調的な物価上昇率が 2％を超えて上振れて
いくリスクが顕在化し、それがその後の経済に悪影響を及ぼすことがないよう、十分に留意す
る必要がある」と指摘した5。 
植田総裁は 9月会合直後の記者会見ですでに、基調的な物価上昇率を 2％程度で安定させて
いくことが重要になったとして、 「政策の局面が変化した」と説明していた。10月会合での文
言変更は、こうした政策局面の変化をより明確にしたものといえる。また、10月 16日の記者
会見でも、今後は基調的な物価上昇率が「これ以上上がるということは容認できない」6と述べ、
2％近辺での定着を目指す考えを改めて示した。 
展望レポートでは、引き続き物価上振れリスクへの警戒を示し、経済・物価・金融情勢に応
じて政策金利を引き上げ、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく方針を前回と同様に明記した。日
銀が物価上振れリスクを見極める上で注視しているのが、①中東情勢に伴う原油価格、②AI関
連需要、③為替相場の3つである。足元ではAI関連投資の収益性への懸念が広がり、円安も一
服しているものの、日銀はこれらの動向が国内物価にどの程度波及するかを引き続き見極めて
いくとみられる。 
展望レポートを見ると、2026年度の実質 GDP成長率は春先以降のエネルギー・原材料価格の
上昇を受けて伸び率を縮小しつつも緩やかな成長を続け、2027年度以降は所得から支出への前

2 日本銀行「総裁記者会見」（2026年9月18日、24日公表） 
3 日本銀行「ETFの売却ペースの見直しに関する決定（2026年 10月）」（2026年 10月30日） 
4 日本銀行「金融市場調節方針の変更について」（2026年9月 18日） 
5 日本銀行「経済・物価情勢の展望（2026年10月）」（基本的見解、2026年10月 30日） 
6 日本銀行「植田総裁記者会見(10月 16日) ―G20終了後の植田総裁、三村財務官 共同記者会見における総裁
発言」（2026年10月 20日）

### Page 3

1

3 / 5

向きな循環メカニズムが強まるもとで成長率を緩やかに高めていくとされた（政策委員見通し
の中央値は2026年度＋0.7％、27年度＋0.7％、28年度＋0.8％） 。 
一方、消費者物価（除く生鮮食品）の前年比は、原油価格の上昇や半導体価格等の上昇、為
替円安などを背景に見通し期間前半に 2％をはっきりと上回る水準まで伸び率を高めた後、後
半には原油高の影響の減衰により、 2％程度に向けてプラス幅を縮小する見通しだ（政策委員
見通しの中央値は 2026年度＋2.5％、2027年度＋2.6％、2028年度＋2.0％） 。夏場以降の原油
価格上昇の影響などから、2027年度が前回（7月）から 0.2％pt上方修正された。消費者物価
（除く生鮮食品・エネルギー）の見通しは 2026年度＋2.4％、2027年度＋2.7％、2028年度＋
2.3％と、前回からの修正は小幅にとどまった。なお、2027年 4月から実施される可能性のあ
る飲食料品の消費税率引き下げは、物価見通しに反映されていない。 
利上げが続く中でも企業・家計への影響はこれまでのところ限定的 
日銀は2024年3月の金融政策決定会合でマイナス金利政策の解除とイールドカーブ・コント
ロール（長短金利操作）の撤廃を決め、その後 5回の利上げや国債買入れの減額を実施するな
ど金融政策の正常化を進めてきた。 
もっとも、企業や家計の経済活動への悪影響はこれまでのところ限定的で、金融環境はなお
緩和的とみられる。まず企業部門を見ると、日銀短観の業況判断DIは高水準を維持しており、
資金繰り判断DIや金融機関の貸出態度判断DIにも目立った悪化は見られない（図表1左）。中
小企業でも顕著な悪化は見られず、利上げが進む中でも、企業の景況感や資金調達環境は総じ
て良好な状態にある。 
ただし、過去の利上げ局面との単純な比較には注意が必要だ。1990年頃までの利上げは、資
産バブル期を含む高成長・高インフレ環境の下で実施されたのに対し、 2000年代以降は低成
長・デフレないし低インフレが長く続き、企業の財務構造も大きく変化した。足元で利上げの
影響が表れにくい背景には、企業の財務体質の改善がある。 
有利子負債から現預金を控除した額の総資産に対する比率（ネット有利子負債比率）は、資
本金 1千万円以上 10億円未満の中小・中堅企業で歴史的な低水準にある（図表 1右）。大企業
では中小・中堅企業ほど低下しておらず、資本金 1千万円未満の企業では相対的に高いものの、
企業部門全体では 1970～90 年代に比べて大幅に低下している。こうした財務構造の変化によ
り、政策金利が上昇しても、企業部門全体では利払い費の増加を通じた金融引き締めの影響が
以前より表れにくくなっていると考えられる。

図表 1：日銀短観の各種 DIの長期推移（左） 、企業規模別に見たネット有利子負債比率（右）

（注）左図は全産業ベースで、破線で示した中小企業は資本金 2千万円以上 1億円未満。 「利上げ局面」は利上
げ開始から利下げ直前までの期間。右図のネット有利子負債比率は、有利子負債から現預金を控除して総資産
で除したもの。資本金 1千万円未満のデータは年度値の平均（その他は四半期値の平均） 。 
（出所）日本銀行、財務省より大和総研作成

家計部門についても、これまでのところ金利上昇の影響は限定的だ。ただし、ストック面か
ら金利上昇への耐性を見ると、借入残高対年収比率は 2000年代初めから大幅に上昇している
（図表 2左）。データが遡及可能な2002年と比べると、2026年上半期の同比率は約53％pt高い。
要因別では、借入残高の増加が同比率を約 66％pt押し上げた一方、年収の増加が約 12％pt押
し下げた。家計の借入負担はストック面で以前より重くなっており、金利上昇が長期化すれば、
その影響が徐々に顕在化する可能性がある。 
図表 2：家計の借入残高対年収比率（左） 、借入返済負担率（右）の2002年からの変化幅

（注）二人以上の勤労者世帯。左図の借入残高対年収比率は、1 世帯あたりの借入残高（「住宅・土地のための
負債」と「住宅・土地以外の負債」の合計）を年間世帯収入で除したもの。右図の借入返済負担率は 1世帯あ
たりの実収入に対する借金返済額の比率。「低所得世帯」は年間収入五分位階級のうち第Ⅰ・Ⅱ分位、「高所得
世帯」は第Ⅳ・Ⅴ分位を集計。「返済世帯比率要因」は、住宅ローン返済世帯の収入が二人以上の勤労者世帯
全体の収入に占める割合の変化による寄与を表す。 
（出所）総務省より大和総研作成

5 / 5

一方、フロー面から足元の資金繰りを見ると、収入に対する借入返済額の比率（借入返済負
担率）は、返済額の減少や収入の増加を背景に、2002年以降の低水準にある（図表2右）。2026
年上半期の借入返済負担率は2002年を約1.1％pt下回った。要因別では、住宅ローン返済世帯
の収入シェア（住宅ローン返済世帯の収入が二人以上の勤労者世帯全体の収入に占める割合）
の上昇が負担率を押し上げた一方、返済額の減少と収入の増加が押し下げた。所得階級別に見
ると、足元では低所得世帯（年収下位 4割）の返済負担率が相対的に上昇しているものの、低
所得世帯、高所得世帯（年収上位4割）ともに2002年の水準を下回る。このため、金利上昇が
家計の資金繰りを大きく圧迫する状況には至っていない。 
ただし、足元の返済負担の低さは今後も続くとは限らない。変動金利型住宅ローンでは、毎
月の返済額の急増を抑える「5 年ルール」や「125％ルール」が広く採用されている7。こうし
た仕組みは、金利上昇の影響が返済額に反映される時期を遅らせ、短期的な負担増を和らげる
半面、負担の顕在化を将来に先送りする。借入残高が高水準にあることも踏まえると、金利高
が長期化するほど家計の返済負担が高まる可能性には留意が必要だ。

中立金利を探りながら、日銀は段階的な利上げを継続へ 
当社では、日銀は次回 2026年 12月の金融政策決定会合で短期金利を1.5％に、2027年 3月
会合で 1.75％に引き上げるとみている。その後は経済・物価・金融情勢への影響を見極めるた
め、いったん政策金利を据え置くと予想する。ただし、1.75％への引き上げ後も金融環境がな
お緩和的で、物価上振れリスクが残る場合には、同年 6月会合で 2.0％への追加利上げに踏み
切る可能性がある。 
日銀は2026年3月に公表したレポート8で、6つのモデルによる自然利子率の推計レンジを▲
0.9％程度～＋0.5％程度と示した。2％の物価上昇率を想定すると、名目の中立金利は 1.1～
2.5％程度で、1.75％はそのレンジのほぼ中央に位置する。だが、中立金利を正確に推計する
ことは困難で、その推計レンジには大きな幅がある9。そのため日銀は、各種データでこれまで
の利上げの影響や物価の基調などを確認しながら、金融緩和の度合いを徐々に調整していくと
みられる。

7 5年ルールは、変動金利型住宅ローンの金利が変動しても、毎月の返済額を5年間据え置く仕組み。125％ル
ールは、返済額の見直し時に、新たな返済額の上限を従来の 125％とする仕組み。いずれも法令で一律に定め
られたものではなく、適用の有無は金融機関や商品によって異なる。 
8 日本銀行「自然利子率の動向と金融緩和度合いの評価」（「日銀レビュー」、2026-J-5、2026年3月） 
9 2026年6月会合後の記者会見で内田眞一副総裁は、中立金利の推計値には「相当なばらつき」があるとした
上で、「短期金利を上げていく過程で、それを含めた金融環境の変化ということを点検して、中立金利の水準
を探りながら、金融緩和の度合いを調整していく」と述べた（日本銀行「総裁記者会見（内田副総裁代理）」
（2026年6月16日、17日公表））。

Document 027: 2026-11-05_［みずほ経済インサイト］欧州大規模デモが問う財政再建の道_c421a30a

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026-11-05_［みずほ経済インサイト］欧州大規模デモが問う財政再建の道_c421a30a.pdf
Report source: Mizuho
Date: 2026-11-05
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

1

欧州大規模デモが問う財政再建の道 
金融市場が動揺するリスクは当面残存する見通し

○ このところ欧州ではインフレに伴う生活苦や公共サービスの劣化等を背景に各地で大規模抗議活
動が発生している 
○ 大規模デモは緊縮を伴う財政健全化を難しくする要因となる。財政懸念や政情不安は国債利回り
の上昇やユーロ安を招いており、経済への悪影響も懸念される 
○ 来年にかけて、欧州では複数の重要な国政選挙が予定される中、政治・財政を巡る不安が欧州金
融市場を動揺させるリスクは当面残存するだろう

１．欧州で広がる大規模抗議活動 
このところ、欧州では大規模デモが多発している（図表1）。フランスでは、9月以降、教員不足や
学校施設の不備など、教育環境の改善を求める高校生の抗議活動が続いていた。こうした中で、10月
半ばには、物価高への対策や公共サービスの改善を求める市民も加わり、抗議活動は全国規模に拡大
した。 
大規模デモが発生しているのはフランスだけではない。フランスのデモに一部触発される形でベル

### Page 2

2

ギーやイタリア等でも抗議活動が発生している。また、スペインでは、高齢女性が家賃を支払えずに
強制退去させられたことをきっかけに、住宅不足や住宅価格・家賃高騰等に対する不満が爆発する形
で大規模な抗議活動が発生し、これが遠因となりサンチェス首相は議会の解散に追い込まれた。 
各地で発生する大規模抗議活動の背景には、近年のインフレによる生活苦やこれを受けた政府への
不満がある1。EUにおいて、生活苦を訴える人の割合は低中所得層を中心に2022年以降高まっている
ほか、 自国が直面する課題としてインフレや経済状況を挙げる人の割合も高い （図表2）。これらの要
因が、自国政府に対する信頼感の低迷につながっている（図表3）。 
本稿では、生活改善を求めて各国で広がる抗議活動が経済や財政、金融市場に与える影響について
考察する。 
２．大規模抗議活動は財政健全化を阻む要因に 
一連の抗議活動は、2018年にフランスで発生した黄色いベスト運動を想起させる2。 当時の参加者は、
自動車に常備が義務付けられている黄色い安全ベストを着用し、燃料税の増税や生活費高騰に抗議し
た。購買力の低下や政府への不満が背景にある点は当時と共通している。異なるのは、政府の追加的
な財政支出の余地が、当時と比べてさらに狭まっていることである。 
欧州債務危機が起きた2010年代前半以降、順調に進捗してきた政府債務の削減は、2020年代に入る
と足踏みしはじめた。パンデミック時の大規模給付やウクライナ戦争後のエネルギー高対策など、危
機対応の財政出動が頻発したためだ。黄色いベスト運動が発生した2018年と比べて政府債務が増加し
たEU加盟国は、27カ国中18カ国にのぼる（図表4）。 
加えて、足元では防衛費増額や社会保障費・利払い負担の拡大など、構造的な財政支出圧力も高ま
っている。 各国の次年度予算案を踏まえると、 財政赤字は当面、高止まりする公算が大きい （図表5） 。
安全保障環境の変化やエネルギー価格の高騰を踏まえ、EUは一定の防衛費・エネルギー関連の追加支
出をEU財政ルールの対象外とする柔軟化措置を設けている。 しかし、 当該措置を適用しているドイツ

### Page 3

3

やベルギーでは、特例対象の追加支出を除いてもなお、財政赤字をGDP比3％に抑制するEUの基準を
超過する見込みである。それでもベルギーでは、連立与党間の予算協議が決裂し、首相が辞意を表明
する事態に陥った。 
フランスを起点に広がった抗議活動は、 緊縮を伴う財政健全化の難しさを浮き彫りにしている。2018
年の黄色いベスト運動では、マクロン政権が燃料税増税の撤回や所得支援策の拡充に踏み切ったこと
で、抗議活動が漸く沈静化に向かった。今回は、11月に新たなデモやストライキが計画されるなど、
いまだ抗議活動が収束する見通しは立っていない。各国政府は事態の長期化を回避するため、家計支
援や公共サービス拡充などの対応を迫られる可能性があり、財政拡張圧力が一段と強まることが懸念
される。 
３．政治・財政を巡る不安が欧州金融市場を動揺させるリスクは当面残存 
金融市場は、欧州主要国の財政状況に対する警戒を強めている。 
フランスをはじめとする高債務国では、政治や財政を巡る混乱を嫌気して、10年債利回りが上昇し
ている（図表6）。2010年代の欧州債務危機を受けてECB（欧州中央銀行）は、条件付きで対象国の国
債を無制限に購入可能なOMT（Outright Monetary Transactions）や経済の基礎的条件では正当化されな
い国債市場の混乱が金融政策の波及を阻害する場合に発動可能なTPI （Transmission Protection 
Instrument）などの市場安定化措置3を整備してきた。当時よりセーフティーネットが整備されている
こともあり、足元の国債利回り上昇が欧州債務危機時のような金融危機につながる可能性は現時点で
は低い。 
とはいえ、金利の上昇は景気下押しや、さらなる財政悪化の要因となる。金利上昇幅が大きいフラ
ンスを例に足元の利回り上昇幅を踏まえて試算すると、設備投資・住宅投資が1年後に3％程度減少す
るとの結果が得られる （図表7） 。金利上昇やそれに伴う景気減速は、利払い費の増加や税収の減少を
図表5 主要国の財政収支実績と見通し 図表6 ユーロ圏の高債務国と 
ドイツの10年債利回り

きなかった国。数値は成立前の政府予算案ベース。

### Page 4

4

通じて、財政をさらに悪化させる要因にもなる。 
各国の政情不安や財政への懸念は、ECBの金融政策運営をも左右する可能性がある。足元では、ユ
ーロが主要通貨に対して下落している （図表8） 。 ユーロ安は、 輸入物価の上昇を通じてインフレ率を
押し上げる要因となる。足元のユーロ下落幅を前提に、時系列モデルを用いてユーロ圏のインフレ率
への影響を試算すると、総合インフレ率は0.3％Pt程度、コアインフレ率は0.1％Pt程度押し上げられる
との結果が得られた（図表9）。 ユーロ圏では、イラン情勢の緊迫化に伴うエネルギー価格の高騰を背
景に、直近でもインフレ率が加速している。先行きユーロ安圧力が強い状況が続けば、インフレ率に
一段の上昇圧力がかかる可能性がある。しかし、ECBはインフレ圧力が一段と強まっても、状況によ
っては追加利上げに踏み切りにくくなることが想定される。利上げがさらなる長期金利の上昇、国債
市場の不安定化を招くおそれがあるためだ。このように政情不安や財政懸念は、ECBの金融政策運営
を困難にする要因にもなりかねない。 
各国の財政や政治情勢に対する金融市場の懸念は当面残存する可能性が高い。欧州では、今年11月
にスペイン議会選挙、来年にはフランス大統領選挙やイタリア議会選挙など、重要な国政選挙が相次
いで予定されている。大規模デモの発生に加えて選挙も控える中、各国政府は国民に負担を求める緊
縮的な財政政策を例年以上に打ち出しにくくなっている。また、各国の選挙では極右政党やポピュリ
スト政党の勢力拡大が予想され、選挙後の政治はより一層不安定化するリスクがある。このほか、コ
ロナ禍からの経済復興を目的に設立されたEU復興基金の受け取りが終了することも、 来年以降の財政
懸念を強める要因になるだろう。同基金の配分が手厚かった南欧や中東欧諸国は、自国の財政負担を
抑えながら公共投資などを通じて経済成長を支えてきたが、来年以降はこうした効果が徐々に剥落す
ることになる。

5

以上のように、2020年代に入ってから、コロナ禍におけるサプライチェーン混乱、ロシアのウクラ
イナ侵攻やイラン情勢の緊迫によるエネルギー高が立て続けに起きてインフレが継続する中、欧州各
国で有権者の生活コスト上昇に対する不満が強まっている。一方、これらの緊急事態に対応するため
財政出動を繰り返してきたことに加え、高齢化に伴う社会保障費の増加、地政学リスクの高まりを背
景とした防衛費増強の必要性、利払い費の増大に直面しており、各国の財政余力は著しく低下してい
る。来年にかけて予定されている主要国の選挙では、極右・ポピュリスト政党の勢力拡大が予想され
ており、政治の混乱に拍車がかかる懸念もある。欧州の政治情勢や財政状況に対する不安が来年にか
けて沈静化するとは考えにくく、国債市場や為替市場が動揺するリスクに引き続き留意する必要があ
ろう。

美期中選舉牽動市場表現，股債配置重品質、控風險
2026 年 11 月 04 日
一、 市場動態
1. 美期中選舉牽動市場情緒，亞股一度隨美股期貨下挫：美國 11 月 3 日舉行期中選舉
，根據 CNN 網站的結果顯示，截至台北時間 11/4 下午 3 點左右，民主黨於參議院
已取得 100 席中的 53 席的過半席次，於眾議院則在 435 席中取得了 225 席(218 席
過半)。市場重新評估國會控制權及川普政府未來政策推動能力，擔心民主黨國會影響
力擴大 ，可能對財政政策及科技企業監管形成新的制衡 。S&P 500 期貨與 Nasdaq 100

期貨在開票期間一度下挫 1.38%及 2.35%，科技股明顯承受較大賣壓。惟隨後跌深反
彈，截至台北時間下午 3 時左右，S&P 500 期貨與 Nasdaq 100 期貨跌幅已收斂至
0.22%及 0.44%。亞洲市場方面，科技股為主及出口導向股市受到美股期貨拖累，台
灣加權指數 11/4 盤中最低下挫 3.38%，所幸隨跌幅收斂，終場下跌 0.83%；韓股同
日也由大跌近 4%由黑翻紅上漲 0.62%。11/3 未開盤的日股，在 11/4 開盤後出現補
跌，最低下挫近 4.8%，但最後跌幅收斂至下跌 1.42%。
2. 美國公債及美元同步反映政策預期調整，市場避險情緒升溫：美國公債及美元反映政
策預期調整，殖利率盤中先跌後漲：10 年期公債殖利率盤中先跌後漲，截至台北時間
下午 3 時來到 4.906%；同一時間，美元指數小跌約 0.37%。股、債、匯市的變動顯
示，投資人除了調整股票風險部位，也在評估未來美國財政刺激規模、政府債務供給
與利率前景。
二、 下跌原因
1. 民主黨取得國會控制權，市場重新定價財政與監管政策：本次選舉對金融市場的主要

影響，在於未來行政與立法部門的權力分配可能改變。共和黨先前同時掌握白宮與國
會，有利於推動稅制、財政及產業政策；民主黨在參眾兩院均取得過半席次後，部分

### Page 2

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
政策將面臨更高的協商成本。財政方面，新增減稅及擴大支出的空間可能受到限制，
市場因而下修部分財政刺激預期；企業方面，國會監督權可能增加對大型科技企業、
AI 資料中心、反壟斷及資料隱私議題的調查壓力。惟國會控制權改變不代表既有政策
立即逆轉，也不代表所有監管措施均能完成立法。此次市場反應更可能是投資人重新
要求政策風險溢酬，而非企業獲利已發生實質下修。
2. AI 資本支出與投資回報疑慮尚未消退，放大科技股對政治事件的反應：此次選舉開票
期間，Nasdaq 期貨及與科技相關的亞股出現大幅波動，亦與近期科技股基本面及評

價爭議有關。10 月底以來美股因大型科技企業 AI 投資效益受到質疑而大幅修正，市
場關注焦點逐漸由 AI 基礎建設支出規模 ， 轉向相關投資能否創造足夠的營運現金流與
股東報酬。以雲端服務及資料中心產業而言，企業雖可透過AI 運算需求增加帶動營收
成長，但 GPU、伺服器、電力設備及資料中心建設需要龐大前期資金，若營收回收速
度不如預期，可能使自由現金流承壓並增加外部融資需求。近期部分科技企業籌資計
畫及財測不佳，已使投資人提高對財務品質的要求。當市場原先已對AI 投資回報存有
疑慮，新的政治及監管不確定性便更容易放大評價修正幅度。
三、 市場看法
1. 半導體需求仍具分化 ， 政治事件不等於AI 產業成長趨勢反轉 ：科技股近期的股價修正
與 AI 終端需求惡化並非相同概念。AMD 近期公布優於市場預期的財報，並對資料中
心業務提出較強勁的成長展望，顯示 AI 運算需求仍具有一定韌性；相對而言，Intel
先前財測疲弱及現金流壓力，則反映不同半導體企業的產品競爭力、資本效率及終端
市場曝險存在明顯差異。因此，不宜將半導體股整體下跌解讀為AI 需求全面降溫。從

產業鏈觀察，具備技術領先優勢、穩定客戶需求及良好現金流的企業，與高度依賴外
部融資、尚未形成穩定獲利模式的基礎建設業者，對資金成本及監管變化的承受能力
並不相同。未來市場可能更加重視個別企業的獲利品質，而非單純以AI 相關營收成長
作為估值依據。
2. 政治事件可能延長科技股評價調整，但中期走勢仍取決於企業獲利：本次期中選舉引
發的期貨跌勢，反映市場對政策方向及國會控制權變化的即時反應，惟政治不確定性
通常須透過實際立法、企業投資計畫或經濟活動變化，才會形成較持久的基本面影響
。從企業獲利角度觀察，路透社引用 LSEG 資料指出，S&P 500 第三季企業獲利仍呈
現強勁成長，科技企業對整體獲利貢獻顯著，顯示目前尚無充分證據支持美國企業基
本面全面轉弱。不過，科技股先前已累積較高成長預期，市場對資本支出回報及監管
風險的容忍度降低，可能使股價即使在營收成長下仍承受評價壓力。短期而言，若選
舉結果逐漸明朗，且企業獲利預估未進一步下修，市場有機會逐步消化政治風險；反
之，若政策不確定性伴隨獲利預期下修，修正時間可能延長。

### Page 3

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
3. 歷史經驗顯示選後股市多正向，後續表現仍宜留意基本面變化：根據彭博統計，1950
年以來美國歷次期中選舉後 12 個月，S&P 500 平均報酬率約 15%，且 19 次全數
收正。其中最高為 1954 年的逾 30%，表現最弱的 1986 年亦小幅上漲。我們認為
，選前政策不確定性消除加上府會分治抑制財政與監管的轉向是為歷史上選後行情的
主要支撐，但相關數據所呈現的為過往經驗，未來股市表現能否延續先前情況仍應觀
察後續基本面變化。
4. 美國公債具避險價值，但利率下行能否持續仍取決於通膨與財政前景：在美國期中選

舉開票期間，美股期貨下跌伴隨美債殖利率回落，顯示公債仍能在股市波動期間提供
一定的風險分散效果。然而，本次殖利率下降可能同時包含避險買盤、財政刺激預期
下修及市場對經濟成長前景重新評估等因素，不宜直接解讀為聯準會即將轉向寬鬆。
尤其能源市場仍受到中東地緣政治及供應中斷風險影響，油價波動可能使通膨前景反
覆，限制長天期公債殖利率的下行空間。若未來財政擴張受到限制，可能對長端利率
形成部分支撐；但若政治僵局提高債務上限協商風險，亦可能使美債期限溢酬波動。
因此，債券配置仍須同時考量收益率、存續期間與通膨風險，不宜僅根據單日行情決
定配置方向。
5. 信用市場是判斷風險是否由股權評價擴散至金融條件的重要指標：目前科技股修正主
要反映評價與政策風險，但若AI 企業持續增加資本支出及外部融資，市場對債務負擔
與償債能力的關注可能進一步升高。對大型投資等級科技企業而言，較穩定的營運現
金流及融資管道有助於吸收資本支出壓力；但對依賴單一客戶合約、設備抵押融資或
高槓桿經營的 AI 基礎建設業者 ， 利率與信用利差上升可能更直接影響投資回報及再融

資能力。後續應追蹤投資等級債與高收益債 OAS、科技及雲端相關發行人利差、新債
發行需求及信用評等展望。若科技股下跌但整體信用利差維持穩定，市場風險可能仍
以股權評價調整為主；若信用利差同步持續擴大，則須提高對金融條件收緊及風險跨
市場擴散的警覺。

不另行通知。
四、 投資建議：
1. 科技股中期成長趨勢仍具支撐，惟短線宜審慎布局並重視個股分化：美國期中選舉帶
來的政策不確定性，短期可能使科技股評價承壓，惟大型雲端業者持續投入AI 基礎建
設，半導體及相關設備需求仍具中期成長動能，目前尚不宜因美股期貨在開票期間的
下挫即全面轉向保守。我們仍建議維持美股核心配置，但短期避免過度集中於高評價
、高資本支出及高度依賴外部融資的企業。產業方面，資訊科技維持選擇性配置，優
先關注具備技術競爭優勢、穩定營運現金流及較高投資回報能見度的企業；對雲端基
礎建設及 AI 融資相關標的，則應提高財務結構與現金流審查標準。

2. 維持中天期美國公債及高信評投資等級債配置，兼顧收益與風險分散：在美國政策風
險升溫及科技股評價調整期間，美國公債仍具投資組合避險功能，但考量通膨與能源
價格的不確定性，現階段不建議延長存續期間。建議以中天期美國公債及高信評投資
等級債作為主要配置方向，兼顧票息收益、利率敏感度與資產配置穩定性。信用債方
面，建議優先選擇營運現金流充足、槓桿適中及再融資需求可控的發行人，對AI 相關
企業則須進一步評估資本支出、合約現金流與債務到期結構。

個股報告

2 群益投顧 
  ESG ：  
環境保護：聯發科技做為全球半導體產業技術領導者，在整體價值鏈中位於
最前端的晶片設計，後端的原物料採購、代工製造、封裝與測試等環節皆交
由供應商執行，形成專業分工與互相合作的夥伴關係。根據風險調查與碳足
跡盤查發現，主要對社會與環境產生衝擊影響的關鍵在於後端製造作業，故
如何與供應商共同合作，以降低社會與環境相關的潛在風險，為聯發科技發
展綠色營運的首要任務。  
社會責任：運用數位科技解決社會問題不是企業的專利，聯發科技相信每個
人都有潛力利用數位科技創造無限可能，聯發科發起數位社會創新競賽 「智
在家鄉」，鼓勵社會大眾為家鄉問題提供解方。  
公司治理：聯發科技董事會下設有審計委員會，強化公司治理獨立性，並設
立薪資報酬委員會，以健全公司薪酬制度為目標，持續強化企業永續發展。
除前述法定功能性委員會外，本公司亦設立法定以外之併購策略委員會，視
實際需求針對集團具體併購案件及整體併購策略評估分析。  
3Q26 產品組合與說明：  
手機晶片占比 37% ，營收 QoQ-1%\YoY-17% ，因搭載 2nm 的天璣 9600 Pro
晶片的手機陸續上市，旗艦級產品表現優於其他產品。  
智慧裝置平台占比 58%，營收 QoQ+20%\YoY+63% ，因 5G 數據晶片導入平
板、 PC、車用座艙等裝置，並且 TV 晶片反映 DRAM 價格。天璣 9500 已搭
載於三星平板。  
PMIC 占比 5%，營收 QoQ+1%\YoY+18% ，因資料中心相關市占率提升。  
AI ASIC 業務的營益率高於公司平均：  
AI ASIC 業務的毛利率低於公司平均，但從 4Q26 開始，對營益率有正向幫
助， AI ASIC 業務的營益率高於公司平均，但由於其他業務有漲價，所以無
法量化差異。  
由於 4Q26 開始，營益率因 AI ASIC 業務已經開始改善，加上相關營收將於
2027 年放量，聯發科認為 2027 年營益率至少能在 20% 以上。  
聯發科第二個 AI ASIC 客戶與專案將於 2028 年開始貢獻營收：  
聯發科原先估第一個 AI ASIC 貢獻 4Q26 營收約 20 億美元，現在上修至貢
獻 25 億美元， 2027 年目標貢獻 160 億美元營收，為 AI ASIC 市場約 20% 市
佔。  
第二個 AI 加速器 ASIC 專案將於 2028 年初量產，此為新的非中系客戶，第
二個專案採用 Multi-die ，一些 die 已經 taped-out ，一些較大顆、較複雜的
die 會晚數周，仍符合時程。  
由於第二個 AI 加速器是採用 Multi-die ，合作對象為美國 IDM 與基板供應
商，聯發科會與台灣、日本供應商一起合作，由於供應鏈還有些變數，聯發
科暫時無法給出 2028 年相關業務的更多細節，目前只表示是非中系客戶。

6 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

In Taiwan, we prefer TUC over EMC for greater pricing upside in
the next 12 months, given its higher exposure to midrange/low-
end CCL. Both stocks are attractively valued: EMC at 11x CY28e
P/E and TUC at 9x.
EMC (2383.TW)
• EMC has introduced a new expansion plan for its total CCL capacity to reach
11.95mn sheets per month (SPM) in 2029 and 15.25mn in 2030 (+14% and

+28% y/y). See Exhibit 3  for EMC's 2026-2030 capacity plans.
• EMC's substrate CCL capacity currently sits at 300K SPM. The company
expects to add another 900K SPM in 2H27, reaching 1.2M SPM by end-2027.
Our supply chain checks suggest that a US hyperscaler  will be the first key
customer of EMC's ABF substrate CCLs for its  eighth-generation ASIC (see
our  TPCA takeaways).
• Management noted a growing trend of customers requesting long-term
agreements that span at least three years, on average. These L TAs now

secure volume rather than fixing prices; they do not require prepayments
from customers. Despite the flexibility in pricing and payment, customers
will still face punishment if they fail to pull their promised volumes.
• We estimate that 4Q revenue will grow ~19% q/q, to NT$72.4bn (+190% y/y),
supported by pricing adjustments and continued mix improvement, given
limited capacity increase in 4Q. We see GM expanding 220bps q/q, to 39.9%,
for EPS of NT$53.33 (+32% q/q).

TUC (6274.TWO)
• Management expects price hikes to continue into 4Q, with new pricing for its
M7/8 CCL taking effect from November onwards. For M6 and below, pricing
will remain driven by raw material cost pass-through amid supply tightness.
We expect this trend to persist into 2027, with HVLP4 copper foil, E glass,
and LDK2 glass cloth to be the most undersupplied.
• We believe recent market concerns about TUC's "share loss" for an ASIC
customer are overdone. Given the ongoing supply tightness in the high-end

CCL market, it is normal for CSPs to adopt multi-sourcing strategies to
diversify supply risks. Meanwhile, we believe TUC is actively engaging and
sampling high-end M8+ grade materials with other major CSP customer(s)
for next-gen AI projects. TUC's share in a key satellite customer's next-gen
satellite products will potentially expand in 2027 as well, with materials
upgraded to M7/8.
• We expect 4Q revenue to grow 15% q/q, to NT$20.6bn (+126% y/y), driven

by pricing adjustments and capacity addition at TUC's site in Thailand site
(+100K SPM in 4Q, +200K SPM in 3Q). We see GM expanding 50bps q/q, to
37.4%, for EPS of NT$15.82 (+20% q/q).

Source: Morgan Stanley Research (E) estimates.
Price Target Discussion and Valuation Methodology
Our price target for EMC increases ~5%, to NT$8,800, driven by increases in our

2026-28 earnings estimates: Our new price target implies 20x CY28e P/E (unchanged).
Our price target is also our base case scenario value. It is derived from our residual income
(RI) model (similar to the rest of our tech hardware coverage).
To calculate the value of the stock, we use the company's beginning equity plus the
present value of all expected residual income of earnings in excess of cost of capital. The
RI is positive when ROAE is above the cost of capital and negative when it is below the

cost of capital. Our key assumptions (all unchanged) include a cost of equity of 9.3% (beta
of 1.0, equity risk premium of 8.7%, and risk-free rate of 1.0%), a medium-term growth rate
of 12%, and a terminal growth rate of 3%.
The 20x CY28 P/E implied by our price target is slightly above the past three years'
average +1SD of 18x, but much lower than the 32x recent P/E peak. We deem this
valuation fair because EMC is benefiting from an earnings growth cycle fueled by stronger

AI server CCL demand, EMC's own mix improvement, and continued pricing adjustments,
which should support margin expansion.
Our bull and bear case values also increase ~5%, to NT$13,650 and NT$4,200,
respectively.

TUC: Earnings Estimate Changes
We raise our  gross margin and operating margin assumptions for CY26-28 to factor in the
margin beat in 3Q26. However, we also revise our tax rate assumptions from 4Q26
onwards to reflect the higher-than-expected tax rate in 3Q26 (31.1% vs. our 25.0%
estimate). Our  EPS estimates consequently change +3% for CY26, -5% for CY27, and -6%
for CY28.

Price target discussion and valuation methodology
Our price target for TUC decreases  ~6%, to NT$2,550, primarily driven by the changes
in our earnings estimates for CY27-28: This implies 28x CY27e or 17x CY28e P/E. Our
price target is also our base case scenario value. It is derived from our residual income (RI)
model (similar to the rest of our tech hardware coverage).
To calculate the value of the stock, we use the company's beginning equity plus the

present value of all expected residual income of earnings in excess of cost of capital. The
RI is positive when ROAE is above the cost of capital and negative when it is below the
cost of capital. Our key assumptions (all unchanged) include a cost of equity of 9.3% (beta
of 1.0, equity risk premium of 8.7%, and risk-free rate of 1.0%), a medium-term growth rate
of 11%, and a terminal growth rate of 3%.
The 17x CY28e P/E implied by our price target is roughly in line with the past three years'

average +1SD of 17.8x. We deem this valuation fair because TUC is benefiting from an
earnings growth cycle fueled by stronger AI server/networking CCL demand, TUC's own
mix improvement, and continued pricing adjustments, which should support margin
expansion.
Our bull and bear case scenario values also decline ~6%, to NT$3,825 and NT$635,
respectively.

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Document 233: [005380] 현대차(005380) 어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력현대차(005380) 어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[005380] 현대차(005380) 어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력현대차(005380) 어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력어려운 업황 속 돋보이는 방어력 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
26년 3분기, 물량 감소에도 확인된 이익 방어력 
현대차의 3Q26 매출액은 45조 1,100억원(Y oY -3.4%), 영업이익은 2조 6,770억원
(Y oY +5.5%), OPM 5.9%(Y oY +0.5%p)를 기록했다. 국내 파업 및 영업일수 감소 영
향으로 글로벌 도매 판매량은 92.1만대(Y oY -11.3%)까지 감소했으나, HEV 중심의 
고부가 차종 Mix 개선, 기말환율 하락에 따른 판매보증비(Y oY -1.2조원) 감소 효과
로 수익성은 오히려 개선됐다. 특히 HEV 판매 비중은 18.2%(Y oY +2.7%p)까지 상
승했다. 반면 EV 판매는 전년 대비 감소세가 이어지며 지역별 수요 둔화와 경쟁 심
화 영향이 지속됐다. 
연간 눈높이는 낮아졌지만, 믹스 개선은 지속 
현대차는 글로벌 수요 둔화와 생산 차질을 반영해 2026년 글로벌 도매 판매 목표를 
기존 415.8만대에서 400만대로, 영업이익률 가이던스를 기존 6.3~7.3%에서 
5.5~6.5%로 하향 조정했다. 다만 북미를 중심으로 HEV 판매가 견조하게 유지되고 
있으며, 하반기 주요 신차 투입과 전동화 라인업 확대를 통해 믹스 개선 기조는 이
어질 전망이다. 중장기적으로는 로보택시 공급 확대와 Atlas 상용화 등 Physical AI 
사업도 계획대로 진행 중으로, 단기 판매 성장 둔화에도 자동차 본업의 수익성 방어
와 신사업 모멘텀은 유효한 것으로 판단한다. 
투자의견 매수 유지, 목표주가 460,000원 하향 
글로벌 완성차 업체들의 수익성 부담은 확대되고 있다. 3분기 자동차 부문 영업이익
률은 Mercedes-Benz 2.5%, Stellantis 0.7%, Volvo -5.6%, VW -7.8%를 기록했다. 
유럽 내 BEV 판매 확대에도 중국 전기차 업체들의 공격적인 해외 진출과 가격 경
쟁, 중국 시장 부진이 동시에 나타나면서 전통 OEM의 수익성 압박이 심화되고 있
다. 이러한 환경에서 현대차는 HEV 중심의 유연한 파워트레인 전략과 북미 중심의 
Mix 개선을 통해 5% 후반의 수익성을 유지하고 있다는 점이 상대적으로 부각된다.

Compliance Notice 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 외부의 압력이나 부당한 간섭없이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
동 자료는 기관투자가 또는 제3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다. 
동 자료는 조사분석자료 작성에 참여한 외부인(계열회사 및 그 임직원등)이 없습니다. 
조사분석 담당자 및 배우자는 해당종목과 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 
동 자료에 언급된 종목의 지분율 1%이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
당사는 상기 명시한 사항 외 고지해야 하는 특별한 이해관계가 없습니다.

Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The U.S. Capex Boom No One is Talking About—And Its Implications
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
We have long been of the belief that the U.S. is on the cusp of an inbound FDI capex boom
that can result in numerous positive spillover effects for the U.S. economy. The latest

figures from the U.S. Department of Commerce bear this out.
Exhibit 1 highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be inside the U.S.,
with FDI totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year. That is a staggering
103% jump from a year ago and places FDI inflows on track for a record year.
The latest inbound capex already adds to the massive scale of foreign investment in the
U.S. To wit, the accumulated stock of FDI in the U.S. stands at nearly $6T, based on the

latest figures from the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA). Manufacturing accounts for
roughly 43% of the total, and, at last count, some 10,000 foreign affiliates were operating
in the U.S.
These firms are largely invisible to the average U.S. investor, but the activities of U.S.
affiliates of foreign multinationals are substantial and competitive- and growth-enhancing.
To this point and based on the latest data from the BEA, foreign affiliates in the U.S.

employed nearly 8.6 million U.S. workers in 2024. That represents 6.1% of total private
sector employment, with manufacturing and retail the two most prominent sectors
of employment. Roughly 24% of all manufacturing jobs in the U.S. are from foreign
subsidiaries. In terms of pay, U.S. workers at these firms make $89,000 annually, 7% higher
than the national average, according to the Global Business Alliance.
Foreign affiliates also contributed some $1.5T to U.S. GDP; comprised some 15.7% of total

U.S. private sector investment; accounted for 12.4% of total U.S. research & development
(R&D) expenditures; paid roughly 20% of all U.S. federal taxes; and made up 22.5% of
U.S. exports and 26.2% of U.S. imports. 1 These are the direct effects of U.S. subsidiaries
of foreign companies operating in the U.S. The indirect effects are even greater in terms
of more income, jobs, growth and tax revenue—all bolstering the long-term competitive
advantage of the U.S. In a nutshell, foreign firms operating in the U.S. are an important

ingredient of U.S. economic growth.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Companies that once served the U.S. market exclusively through exports
increasingly face higher costs and more uncertainties and hence want to be “inside.”
Two, with a $32T-plus economy with extraordinary consumer purchasing power, foreign

firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment than just trade (exports). There
is no more dynamic consumer in the world than the American shopper.
Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers, semiconductors, power
generation, etc., has created numerous investment opportunities for many foreign firms
that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging capex cycle.
Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly developed

technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.
As more U.S. affiliates of foreign multinationals set up shop in the U.S., think more job
creation across the country, more manufacturing output, more locally sourced purchases,
more U.S. exports, more spending on R&D, and more tax revenue for local communities.
And over the long run, think a more globally competitive U.S. economy.
1 Activities of U.S. Affiliates of Foreign Multinational Enterprises, 2024” and “Activities of U.S. Affiliates of Foreign

Multinational Enterprises, 2023”, BEA July 2026.
Investment Implications
The boom in foreign investment in
the U.S. should continue to support
employment, productivity, corporate
earnings, and broader U.S. economic
competitiveness, in our view. Hence
our U.S. bias.
Exhibit 1: FDI Inflows to the U.S.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
And speaking of global competitiveness, Exhibit 2A is very illustrative of the magnet pull of
the U.S. economy to attract FDI versus the rest of the world. Of new greenfield investment
this decade, note the overriding preference for the U.S., accounting for nearly 16% of total
global greenfield investment this decade. America’s share is larger than the next three
countries combined—the United Kingdom, India, and Germany. And note that America’s

share of new investment is more than 5X that of China’s. Most of this new investment
has gone into manufacturing sectors like electrical equipment, computers, appliances and
batteries and has originated from Asia and Europe.
The bulk of foreign investment comes from Europe, followed by Japan and Canada, but as
Exhibit 2B highlights, there is a new dynamic playing out when it comes to America’s ability
to attract capital. There is a new source in town: a handful of developing nations (namely

Taiwan, South Korea, Mexico and Brazil). Mexican manufacturers, Taiwan technology
leaders, South Korea chip leaders, Brazil industrial firms, India pharmaceutical firms—these
companies and more are realizing that in today’s trade environment, the best way to serve
the American market is not necessarily through exports but through investment—investing
locally. FDI flows from developing nations reached $63B in the 12 months through June,
more than double the level a year prior ($24.5B), according to the BEA. Fueled by insatiable

AI demand, flows from South Korea alone totaled $23.4B in the first half of 2026. The
upshot is that an increasingly important source of capital for the U.S. is coming from an
unlikely source: the developing nations.
More fire for the capex boom. The coming boom in FDI inflows is supportive of our thesis
that the U.S. stands on the cusp of a major industrial upgrade. We are in the early innings
of a structural—not cyclical—capex cycle that should lead to the rebuilding of domestic

supply chains, higher value-added manufacturing, and more AI-driven productivity growth.
Foreign firms, in our opinion, will be part of this process.
That is important to recognize because remember the following: when a foreign company
builds a factory or an R&D facility or expands its existing manufacturing capacity right
here in the U.S., good things happen. Demand for U.S. goods and services goes up (as does
demand for construction, materials, logistics and professional services). New jobs are created,

boosting incomes of U.S. workers, while supporting more local spending and consumption.
And because many foreign firms often bring advanced manufacturing technologies when
investing in the U.S., the end results include a boost in productivity and innovation in various
sectors of the economy.
All the above, of course, are the secret ingredients to stronger U.S. economic growth, rising
corporate earnings and higher equity prices. With a little help from our friends, America’s

industrial renewal is gaining strength and momentum.

4 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Europe’s Electoral Cycle and Market Risks
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
As midterm elections approach for the U.S. next month, voters across Europe will also
enter their own election season starting this November and continuing throughout
2027. Over the next year, 52% of the total EU population, representing 46% of EU GDP
across eight countries, will go to the polls, including voters in three of its four largest

economies (Exhibit 3). Having announced snap general elections earlier this month,
Spain will vote for members of its lower and upper house on November 29. A series of
parliamentary elections are then due for 2027 in Estonia (by March 7), Finland (on April
18), Greece (by July 25), Slovakia (by September 28), Poland (by November 11) and Italy
(by December 22). And perhaps most consequentially France will stage Presidential
elections on April 18, with a potential second round of voting set for May 2.

For the EU as a whole, the significance of these coming elections will be the potential
for a shift toward more populist, domestically oriented policy priorities from leading
opposition candidates (particularly in France, Spain and Poland) which could obstruct the
process of closer EU integration. In the nearer term, adoption of the new seven-year
(2028-2034) EU budget due by the end of 2027 could be impeded. And over the longer
term, renewed efforts toward an EU-wide capital market union to deepen and attract

more inward investment into European markets could also be further delayed.
At the country level, investors have seen bond yields rise globally in 2026 on the back of
higher inflation and concerns over fiscal sustainability. The debt question has arguably
been most pressing in parts of Europe, especially given the likelihood that upcoming
elections make for domestic increases in government largesse. And of the eight
countries going to the polls over the next year, France remains the biggest source of

concern. Interest rate spreads on French 10-year sovereign bonds have overtaken those
in both Greece and Italy this year (Exhibit 4), driven by investor concerns over public
debt. The 2027 government budget unveiled earlier this month projected a deficit of
5.0% of GDP (well in excess of the 3% specified in the EU Stability and Growth Pact),
but remained insufficiently expansive to nullify the risk that the current government
could be unseated later this year if it is forced through the fragmented parliament. This

comes on top of an existing debt load of close to 120% of GDP and a halving of official
2026 growth forecasts from 1.0% at the start of the year to 0.5% in September. In
short, all four variables in France’s debt sustainability equation currently stand as major
headwinds, and the two leading candidates in the 2027 election have shown limited
appetite for fiscal consolidation over the coming years.

We remain cautious on European
markets overall, despite seeing
pockets of strength in selected
areas such as defense, data center
infrastructure and renewable energy.
On top of growing uncertainty
from the upcoming electoral cycle

and interest rate outlook, rising
competition and slowing demand
from China continue to challenge
key segments of the Consumer
Discretionary sector. And limited
direct AI concentration also leaves
Europe under-exposed to the key
growth theme that continues to buoy
equity markets in the U.S., Japan and
emerging Asia.

Intervention from the ECB under its 2022 bond purchase program is also unlikely at this

stage given that the spread widening in France has not appeared either disorderly or
unwarranted. Meanwhile, conditions could potentially be aggravated by expected ECB rate
hikes in response to still above-target inflation across the eurozone. Indeed recent moves
to tighten monetary policy from a number of major central banks have been read most
hawkishly in Europe. Most of the increase in European bond yields has come from higher
inflation breakevens as opposed to real yields in the U.S. and Japan. And despite some

paring back of tightening expectations in the wake of recent bond volatility, the market is
still pricing three more policy rate increases over the next 12 months.
Euro depreciation and an emerging shift toward more protectionist trade policy with China
could further complicate the ECB policy outlook. China posted a record $35B trade surplus,
according to Eurostat, with the EU in September (alongside its $938B total surplus for the
first three quarters of 2026), and the two markets have agreed to reduce the EU bilateral

deficit by capping imports of hybrid cars for four years. Hybrids account for less than 2%
of total EU imports from China, but this could potentially mark the start of a broader shift
toward more trade protection in Europe with its largest foreign supplier which risks adding
further upward pressure to cost-push inflation. And though the leading candidates in the
French election may favor resuming oil and gas imports from Russia, Europe also remains

among the most at risk from the global energy shock. Indeed some 56% of the region’s
crude oil consumption is in the form of diesel product according to the International Energy
Agency, more than twice the U.S. share.
The 2- to 10-year eurozone yield curve has already flattened significantly since the start
of the year. And at just over 30 basis points as of mid-October, it remains flatter than
its equivalents in the U.S., UK and Japan. Outside Energy and Technology, Financials have

been among the strongest-performing sectors in Europe this year. But additional ECB
rate hikes could lead to further curve flattening, compressing bank net interest margins
and contributing to slower growth—particularly given that rate increases are coming in
response to supply-driven inflation rather than reflecting stronger economic activity.
We nonetheless continue to see pockets of strength in European markets. Growing
defense budgets should remain a tailwind for Industrials as national governments look to

meet their North Atlantic Treaty Organization targets over the next several years. And the
sector should be further supported by indirect exposure to AI investment through electric
cabling, power infrastructure and building materials for data centers. Leaders in renewable
energy equipment and services are also likely to be beneficiaries of grid upgrades and
broadening electrification globally, particularly given elevated fossil fuel prices. But our view

on the region remains cautious overall. On top of growing uncertainty from the upcoming
electoral cycle and interest rate outlook, rising competition and slowing demand from
China continue to challenge key segments of Europe’s Consumer Discretionary sector.
Limited direct AI concentration in technology hardware, cloud services and application
software also leaves Europe underexposed to the key growth theme that continues to buoy
equity markets in the U.S., Japan and emerging Asia.

### Page 6

6 of 8 October 19, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Cautious Optimism Entering Fiscal Year 2027
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
“A renewed focus on rebuilding fiscal space is urgent, given elevated debt, higher
borrowing costs, multiplying demands on public budgets and heightened uncertainty.” So
warned the IMF about the global fiscal outlook last week in its updated World Economic

Outlook report. Much is at stake for developed nations in Europe and Asia facing high
existing debt, unfavorable demographics and more modest growth.
For the U.S., between structurally higher mandatory outlays, the administration’s $1.5T
proposed defense package, and the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield sitting above 5%, spending
is likely headed higher. We continue to fade concerns of an impending U.S. debt crisis,
however, largely because economic growth continues to surprise to the upside. Expanding

output is good news for the debt-to-GDP ratio, which remains a touch below 100%.
Indeed, rather than being led higher on account of un-anchored inflation expectations, the
10-year U.S. Treasury yield has repriced to reflect better-than-expected nominal growth
(Exhibit 5A). Higher yields in the face of slowing growth, meanwhile, would be a different
story, not only for the fiscal outlook (worse) but also for U.S. equity valuations (lower).
Of course, after the U.S. accumulated another $2T deficit in FY 2026, 2 “rebuilding fiscal

space” in the IMF’s words will be politically unpopular and mathematically challenging.
In our view, the biggest near-term tail risk would be a surprise move lower in growth as
budget deficits at 6% of GDP in good times leave little room for bad times. This isn’t our
base case given robust consumer spending per our internal credit and debit card data and
ongoing investment in the AI buildout and beyond. We expect these two pillars to sustain
growth in 2027.

The other good news is that, contrary to concerns, demand for Treasurys remains robust.
Yields are attractive—that was the message from October’s successful $39B 10-year
Treasury note auction. At the same time, U.S. debt boasts an array of buyers: domestic
households, the Federal Reserve, mutual funds and exchange-traded funds, money market
funds, pension funds, foreign central banks, private investors overseas, and more. Per
Exhibit 5B, whereas foreign official investors once dominated some 40% of the U.S.

Treasury market during the 2008/2009 Great Financial Crisis, their share has fallen to
12% while domestic private investors now account for a majority of holdings. Translated:
demand is more diverse than most appreciate.

Investment Implications
In our view, higher nominal and real
yields present a compelling entry point
for investors seeking income and can
provide cushion in the face of higher
rate volatility. We expect demand for
U.S. assets to remain healthy and favor
the U.S. over developed economies in
Europe facing more modest growth and
fiscal instability.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• We've lifted our Q3 expectation for real PCE on incoming data and a slightly firmer consumer
backdrop in the wake of recent revisions.
Consumer spending looks firmer in the third quarter, and we now expect real PCE to rise at a 3.4%
annualized clip. This upward revision reflects recent data, with August consumption coming in firm
with real spending rising by the fastest monthly clip in nearly two years, but also reflects a slightly

firmer consumer backdrop in the wake of the BEA's annual revisions released late September. Although
revisions did not materially alter spending, they lifted the level of household income in recent years,
which also boosted the personal saving rate. The trajectory of real income growth remains weak, but
the consumer still looks to be in a slightly stronger financial position than previously believed, which
helps explain recent resilience in spending and suggests consumers may have a bit more runway ahead.

Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build out will lead to another surge in business fixed investment spending in Q3, though the
effect on headline growth will be partly offset by stronger imports.
Business fixed investment spending continues to be supported primarily by the high-tech buildout.
We forecast real equipment investment to rise at around a 16% annualized clip in Q3, but part of
this boost to headline GDP growth will be offset by stronger import growth that is facilitating the

build out. Net imports of computers are tracking to surge in the third quarter, which will provide a
sizable lift to information processing equipment investment, or around half of overall equipment.
And while non-defense capital goods shipments (including aircraft) slipped in August, the near-term
growth trajectory remains supportive of a pickup in equipment. The rebuild in inventories we have
been anticipating is also tracking to be a bit stronger as businesses replenish drawn down product.

Investment: Residential
• The abrupt rise in mortgage rates is likely to dampen residential investment over the next few
quarters. Residential spending should find support next year as longer-term interest rates
moderate somewhat.
We are unlikely to see another positive residential investment reading in Q3. Rising mortgage rates

have sent a chill over builder outlooks, pushing sales expectations to their weakest level in more than
three years. While builders have responded with deeper price cuts, affordability pressures continue
to weigh on single-family sales and construction outlays. Apartment construction is simultaneously
soft amid rising financing costs and modest rent growth, and home improvement spending remains
constrained by high materials costs and interest rates. While residential investment is likely to remain

4 | Economics

### Page 5

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
a drag this year, we expect a slight moderation in longer-term interest rates in 2027, which would
provide a more supportive backdrop.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures
• We have not made material changes to our nonresidential structures forecast. Despite elevated
interest rates, data center and related spending should continue to support structures investment
through our forecast horizon.
Nonresidential structures spending has accelerated sharply, overwhelmingly driven by data centers

and electric power facilities. As data center construction expands geographically and demand rises
for semiconductors, high-tech capex has also started to generate positive spillovers for warehousing
and manufacturing construction. This buildout, which appears relatively insensitive to interest rates,
should be enough to sustain solid growth in structures investment even as spending across most
other categories remains essentially flat. Domestic energy is another green shoot, with elevated prices

supporting ongoing growth in active rotary rigs.

Net Exports & Trade
• An expected drag from trade in Q3 got larger in light of a near-record trade deficit in August, net
exports is now expected to lop off three full percentage points from third quarter growth.

Exports increased in August for the first time in four months, but the increase was more than offset by
an even larger increase in imports. The upshot was a widening in the trade deficit to a near-record gap
of more than $105 billion. Going into the report, we had a 1.9 percentage point drag from net exports
penciled in for Q3 GDP; we revised that to a full 3.0 percentage points drag in light of the record gap.
Even a modest narrowing in the final months of the year would likely mean a lift to headline GDP in

Q4, but the real story here is that trade will remain a headwind throughout the forecast period owing
largely to surging imports of tech-related products amid the AI boom.
Labor Market
• The jobs market remains stable. We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end the year at
4.2% and hold near that level in 2027.
We modestly revised down our near-term payroll growth forecast following the September jobs
report, but otherwise we have left our labor market outlook unchanged. Measures of labor demand

growth continue to point to a stable labor market. Job openings have inched higher on a year-ago
basis but remain broadly steady while initial jobless claims, WARN notices and Challenger job cut
announcements continue to signal low layoffs. Labor force participation appears to be improving after
its sharp pullback in the first half of the year. Looking ahead, we expect payroll growth to average
around 60K per month in 2027, roughly in line with our estimate of breakeven job growth. That should

Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
keep the unemployment rate near 4.2% in 2027 and leave labor market conditions broadly consistent
with "full employment." Notably, our expectation for labor cost growth of ~3.4% is the reason we
continue to reiterate that labor cost growth is not a contributor to the excess inflation seen in the
data.
Inflation
• We expect the core PCE deflator to rise 3.2% and 2.6% Q4/Q4 in 2026 and 2027, respectively.
Headline inflation remains heavily influenced by developments in the Middle East, with higher oil

prices lifting gasoline and diesel prices. Food inflation has been tame this year but looks poised to
strengthen as higher transportation and commodity prices work their way through supply chains.
Beyond food and energy, price pressures remain concentrated in a handful of categories, most notably
airfares and AI-related tech goods. Core goods inflation is showing signs of stabilizing, as waning tariff-
related price pressures have been offset by resilient demand tied to the AI buildout. We expect some

improvement here in 2027. Still-low shelter inflation and cooler price growth for airfares and health
care should help bring about some moderation in core services inflation next year. We expect the core
PCE deflator to rise 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis in 2026 before slowing to 2.6% in 2027.

Fiscal Policy
• Fiscal year (FY) 2026 ended on September 30, and the federal government tallied a $2.0 trillion
budget deficit (6.5% of GDP). We project the deficit to be unchanged in dollar terms in FY 2027

and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).
• Another continuing resolution to extend government funding past the December 11 deadline
seems like the most likely outcome at this time for the FY 2027 budget. It is possible Republicans
use the lame duck session of Congress to increase defense spending and raise the debt ceiling.
Looking ahead to the lame duck session of Congress that will follow the midterm elections, Congress
needs to pass a bill that extends government funding past December 11 to avert a government

shutdown. Another continuing resolution that punts the issue into Q1 seems like the most likely
outcome. Perhaps more interesting will be whether Republicans attempt to use budget reconciliation
to pass one more partisan piece of legislation. If they do, we suspect the two things at the top the
agenda will be more funding for national defense and immigration enforcement priorities as well as a
debt ceiling increase. For the latter, raising it in the lame duck would remove some leverage Democrats

could have in 2027 if they recapture one or both chambers. It is still very early, but we estimate the
debt ceiling "X date" by which Congress must act or face default is sometime in late Q3/early Q4 of
next year.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• Our base case forecast is for the FOMC to hike the fed funds rate by 25 bps in December and
then keep the policy rate unchanged in 2027 at 4.00%-4.25%, with risks skewed toward additional
tightening.
As we go to print, the FOMC seems more likely than not to keep the fed funds rate unchanged in
October. But given the rationale under which the Fed started this tightening cycle and the forcefulness

with which Warsh has driven home his displeasure with elevated inflation, it seems entirely plausible
to us that October will be a live meeting. At a minimum, the recent inflation data seem to make a hike
in December a lock. Past December, the outlook will be highly dependent on future inflation data. Our
expectation is that core inflation will cool gradually in 2027, and we still do not see signs of demand-
side inflation in the data. But hiking cycles have an inertial component to them, and the recent inflation

data reset the clock to zero on the Fed seeing several good inflation prints in a row. This creates upside
risk to our base case for the fed funds rate.

International Developments
• Global growth remains resilient, leaving our 2026 and 2027 forecasts unchanged. Our inflation
forecasts are also unchanged, although risks of delayed price pass-through could prolong inflation
pressures into 2027.

• Monetary policy continues to lean toward tightening across advanced economies, while emerging
market central banks are increasingly cautious, either holding rates or easing more gradually as
inflation pressures rebuild.
The balance of risks to global inflation remains tilted to the upside, complicating the monetary policy
outlook even as growth has proven more resilient than expected. Elevated energy prices, particularly
for refined petroleum products, persistent geopolitical uncertainty and El Niño-related food price

risks could prolong inflationary pressures into 2027. While second-round effects still remain relatively
contained, repeated supply shocks are testing central banks’ willingness to look through the temporary
price increases, particularly where inflation expectations remain elevated, even as higher government
bond yields are already contributing to tighter financial conditions.
Across advanced economies, central banks face increasingly difficult trade-offs as persistent inflation

pressures limit the scope for policy accommodation. The Eurozone remains particularly vulnerable
to energy supply disruptions, with elevated natural gas prices and low storage levels adding to
inflation risks. We continue to expect a 25 bps European Central Bank hike in December, followed by a
prolonged hold through 2027. Fiscal concerns are also adding to pressure, particularly in France, where
the selloff in government debt has pushed yields higher. In the U.K., resilient activity and renewed

Economics | 7

### Page 8

Monthly Economics
inflation pressures similarly underpin our expectation for a Bank of England hike in November,
followed by a hold through 2027.
Elsewhere, policy paths are becoming more differentiated. After four hikes this year brought the Cash
Rate to 4.60%, we expect the Reserve Bank of Australia to remain on hold, although renewed inflation
pressures or stronger-than-expected activity could prompt further tightening. We also still expect
the Bank of Canada to hold at 2.25%, with subdued growth weighing against energy-driven inflation

risks, although incoming inflation data over the coming weeks will be critical in determining whether
persistent price pressures warrant tightening monetary policy perhaps as early as the December
meeting. Japan remains an exception to the broader hold outlook: while the yen's recovery provides
some relief from imported inflation, elevated energy prices and import dependence continue to pose
upside risks. We expect the Bank of Japan to hike again by year-end and now expect an additional 25

bps hike in Q3-2027, bringing the terminal rate to 1.75%.
The emerging market story has increasingly shifted from easing toward patience or selective
tightening. In Asia, China's weak domestic demand and persistent property-sector strains continue to
warrant policy support, with AI-related exports providing some offset. We expect a more gradual pace
of Reserve Requirement Ratio cuts alongside targeted monetary and fiscal measures. India faces the

opposite challenge, with elevated food and energy prices and rupee depreciation strengthening the
case for further Reserve Bank of India tightening. In Latin America, peso depreciation and elevated
energy costs pose upside inflation risks for Mexico, although limited exchange-rate pass-through
should allow Banxico to remain on hold while policymakers monitor underlying inflation and inflation
expectations. In Brazil, election-related uncertainty and renewed inflation pressures are likely to delay

further easing, with cuts expected to resume in Q1-2027 once policymakers see more convincing
evidence of disinflation.

### Page 1

#inflation #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ Consumers Price Index Q3 2026 and change
of OCR call
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
09:19 October 22 2026 Share
Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter and 3.7% y/y. Core inflation
pressures are elevated and market pricing is primed for a 25bp OCR hike next week, which
we now expect.
Consumers Price Index, September quarter 2026 – key stats

### Page 2

Headline inflation
Quarterly change: +0.7% (prev: +1.5%)
Westpac forecast: +0.7%
RBNZ: +0.8%
Market median: +0.8%, range +0.5% to +1.2%

Annual change: +3.7% (prev: +4.1%)
Westpac forecast: +3.7%, RBNZ: +3.9%, Market: +3.8%

Non-tradables
Quarterly change: +1.0% (prev: +0.6%)
Westpac forecast: +1.3%
Annual change: +3.3% (prev: +3.4%)

Tradables
Quarterly change: +0.2% (prev: +2.7%)
Westpac forecast: -0.2%
Annual change: +4.3% (prev: +4.9%)

Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter. That saw the annual inflation rate easing to
3.7%, down from 4.1% in the year to June. 
Today’s result was in line with our forecast, but lower than the RBNZ’s forecast for 3.9% annual
inflation. 
However, under the surface, the details were more concerning. Measure of core inflation have taken a
step higher, with several closely watched measures now at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s
target band. 
We suspect that today’s result will leave the market continuing to price a strong chance of another
OCR hike at next Wednesday’s policy review. In that light, we think the RBNZ will regard this as the
path of least resistance, and we now expect that they will deliver (previously we expected that they
would pause and assess, before hiking at the December MPS).

What contributed to inflation in the September quarter?
Underpinning September’s 0.7% rise in consumer prices were some big price movements in both
directions. 
The largest move in prices this quarter was actually a sharp fall in fuel costs. Fuel prices aren’t low by
any stretch of the imagination. However, average prices for both petrol and diesel over the September
quarter were lower than in the June quarter when prices rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle East

### Page 3

conflict. In terms of specifics, the average price for 91 unleaded was 6.5% lower in September than in
the June quarter, while diesel prices were close to 16% lower. Those moves took 0.4ppts off inflation
this quarter. 
While prices at the pump weren’t as high as they were in the June quarter, fuel costs are still painfully
high. And that’s been bleeding through into higher prices for other goods and services. Of note, the
September quarter saw large increases in passenger transport costs, with international airfares up 14%
and domestic airfares up nearly 8%. 
The September quarter also saw the annual increases in local council rates (up 6.2%) and charges for
services like water supply (up 7.5%). That continues the run of large increases seen in recent years.
Lastly, the past few months saw a 1.0% rise in food prices, largely as a result of the seasonal rise in
prices for fresh vegetables. 
On the downside, we’ve seen continued softness in housing rents, which are the largest component of
the CPI, accounting for 11.2% of the basket. With low population growth and ample supply, rents were
flat in the September quarter and have effectively been flat since the start of this year. In annual terms,
rental inflation is the lowest it’s been in more than two decades, with regions like Wellington seeing
outright falls.

Annual and core inflation 
The annual inflation rate dropped back to 3.7% in the September quarter, down from 4.1% in the year
to June and the highest level in two years.
Looking under the surface, prices in the domestically oriented non-tradables group rose 1.0% over the
quarter, less than expected. That saw annual non-tradables inflation nudging down to 3.3% (from 3.4%
in the year to June). 
Non-tradables inflation has continued to run at above-average rates over the past year. That’s despite
the softness in economic growth and the labour market. In large part, that’s due to the continued
sizeable increases in administered prices, such as electricity charges which rose 9.3% over the past
year. 
However, prices in other areas have also been firm. Non-tradables excluding housing and utilities costs
(which also omits local council rates) was 3.4% for the year. In seasonally adjusted terms, quarterly
non-tradables inflation has been tracking around 0.8% for most of the past year. It was noted that in
sectors like construction, higher materials costs are adding to prices. Feedback from business we've
heard indicates that those higher materials costs are likely related to high oil prices. 
On the imported front, lower fuel costs saw tradable prices rising by just 0.2% over the September
quarter (though above our forecast for a small fall). That saw annual tradables inflation dropping back
to 4.3%, down sharply from the 4.9% rise in the year to June. 
While fuel costs have been a key driver for tradables inflation, under the surface, other imported
inflation pressures are looking firm. Excluding food and fuel costs, tradables prices were up 2.7% over
the past year. That’s the highest level in three years. 
With big swings in fuel costs, and concern that this is spilling over into operating costs more generally,
a key focus for the RBNZ is what’s been happening to measures of core inflation (which smooth
through quarter-to-quarter swings in individual prices and instead track the underlying trend in

### Page 4

Related articles
inflation). 
On this front we did some see worrying signs. Several closely watched measures of core inflation have
picked up, and now sit at the top of (or slightly above) the RBNZ target band. In terms of specifics, we
saw: 
Reinforcing this picture of firm underlying inflation pressures, a growing number of goods have been
increasing in price. That could be an early indication that businesses, even those with a limited
exposure to fuel costs, are starting to factor in expectations of continued high inflation when setting
prices.

Outlook 
While today’s result was lower than the RBNZ assumed in their September MPS projections, the
firmness in core inflation will give the RBNZ pause for thought ahead of their policy review on 28
October. Furthermore, with a sharp rise in petrol prices in recent weeks, September’s easing in annual
inflation is likely to be just a temporary reprieve. We expect headline inflation to reaccelerate back over
4% before the end of this year, and it’s set to remain above the RBNZ’s current forecast for most of
2027. 
Market pricing and views have coalesced around an October hike which we now suspect the MPC will
see as the path of least resistance. Core inflation measures are now close to or above 3%, which will
add fuel to arguments from the MPC hawks. We will have more detail in our MPR Preview later today,
including on the implications for the shape of the tightening cycle moving forward.

SPECIAL EDITION | HOW BAS IS THE RISE

TRANSCRIPTION

0:00:10 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Everyone, welcome. As we head into autumn 2026, interest rates are reaching levels never seen for many, many years. 
Why is this happening? Is it sustainable? And what are the implications for the economies of different countries? All these 
questions will be discussed over the next few minutes with the economists of BNP Paribas. Let us begin with Isabelle 
Mateos y Lago, chief economist of the group. Hello, Isabelle.  
0:00:38 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everyone. 
0:00:39 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you for being with us today. Just to begin, a simple explanation. What rates are we talking about and which countries 
are affected? 
0:00:48 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, it is very important to clarify this. So, we are talking about long-term interest rates, rates at 10 years or longer 
maturities, 30 years, etc. The rates at which sovereign states borrow on the market. And this is primarily something that is 
happening in advanced countries, so United States, United King dom, Japan, and then the Eurozone countries. And indeed, 
as you said, we are seeing since the beginning of the summer, interest rates reach levels we had not seen for about 20 
years. But it is important to keep in mind that up until that point, up until the mid -2000s, the level of interest rates that 
we're seeing now were really quite common, quite normal, so one shouldn't overdramatize.  
0:01:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why do we find ourselves in this situation and how did we get to this point?  
0:01:41 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
Yes, so the direct cause is that investors are demanding higher interest rates in order to  lend their capital to governments. 
But why is this? Well, there's essentially three reasons for this, and not all of them are bad, and they're not mutually 
exclusive either. The top reason to my mind is that there's just greater competition for capital be tween sovereign states 
and the corporate sector, who are all facing simultaneously additional investment needs, in defense, in artificial 
intelligence, in energy transition, in resilience. And so,  you just have greater competition for capital and that pushes up the 
price of capital, which is the interest rate. 
Reason number two is because markets have revised upwards their expectations of how high central banks are going to be 
taking their interest rates. 
And this mechanically translates into higher rates at the long end. And then there's a third reason, which doesn't apply 
necessarily in every case, but which is essentially higher sovereign risk, a kind of a risk premium, in cases where there are  
concerns arising about the sustainability of these public debts.

### Page 2

2

0:02:59 - Emmanuel Laborde 
What are the implications for global economies? 
0:03:03 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago 
So that's exactly what we'll be discussing in the next segments. But basically, the two key issues are, one, an adverse 
impact on public finances. It's higher interest rates making it harder to balance the books in terms of public accounts. And 
then secondly, these higher rates tend to slow down economic growth. Of course, by how much and how serious it will be, 
depends on how high interest rates go and how long they stay there. But that's for the next segments.  
0:03:38 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Yes. Thank you very much, Isabelle. Having said the scene now, let's move on to the first segment with the following 
question. What makes debt sustainable? And let's see what Hélène Baudchon has to say.  
Hello Hélène and welcome into this special edition. 
0:03:58 - Hélène Baudchon 
Hello Emmanuel, hello everybody. 
0:04:01 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When economists talk about public debt, they often refer to the sustainability of debt. As an economist, can you explain to 
us what that means and what is the debt trajectory deemed unsustainable?  
0:04:15 - Hélène Baudchon 
To begin with, I will say that a temporary increase in debt can be seen as a normal evolution, not particularly concerning, 
for instance during an economic crisis or more recently during a health crisis. In such cases, it acts as a buffer. A problem  
arises when the debt level never comes back down. An unsustainable trajectory can be therefore defined, characterized by 
a continuous regular rise in the debt level, both in absolute terms and as a proportion of GDP, driven by high and recurring 
fiscal deficits. And sometimes interest payments can exacerbate the problem.  
0:05:00 - Emmanuel Laborde 
In very simple terms, you are talking about the snowball effect.  
0:05:03 - Hélène Baudchon 
Exactly explaining why consolidation measures are needed in order to prevent such an adverse situation from occurring 
and in order to prevent, in fact, a sudden loss of confidence from creditors.  
0:05:16 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Regarding governments, what should serve as a warning sign for them? And at what point could the situation become, let's 
say, problematic? 
0:05:27 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, simply speaking, when interest rates rise, not so long ago, up until 2022, Eurozone countries were borrowing at very 
low rates and in some instance, even negative rates. And although debt levels were already at high level at that time, the 
low rates helped manage debt servicing. Not much fiscal effort was needed to keep the public debt ratios in check.  
0:05:55 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Of course, we all remember this because it was a very unusual situation, but now things are very different.

### Page 3

3

0:06:01 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed, the situation today is clearly more challenging simply because bond yields are significantly higher and they 
are rising at the time of our discussion. This is for the part the result of the ECB's monetary tightening in 2022 -2023 aimed 
at addressing the inflation shock then. Then long-term interest rates have risen as a consequence and have aligned more 
closely with GDP growth rate. And in technical terms, this is what we refer to as a narrowing of the "r" - "g" spread. 
0:06:41 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Why is this spread so important? Why do you, the economists, monitor it this way?  
0:06:48 - Hélène Baudchon 
Essentially, you have the interest rate "r", which determines the debt burden, while the growth rate "g" determines the 
trajectory of fiscal revenues. And if "r" is lower than "g", and this has been the case for many years, as you can see on the  
chart on the screen, it means that interest payments are increasing at a slower pace than the revenue component of the 
equation. 
0:07:14 - Emmanuel Laborde 
And this provides some leeway in other budgetary areas, such as education, of course, defense, maybe pensions.  
0:07:22 - Hélène Baudchon 
Yes, indeed. And this is what we refer to as the primary budget. However, if and when interest rates do go up while the 
economy isn't growing that much faster, then fiscal leeway diminishes.  
0:07:35 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Crystal clear. Now, just could you provide a few last words to wrap up the sustainability segment?  
0:07:43 - Hélène Baudchon 
Well, to conclude, we are entering a period which demands greater fiscal discipline or, as some might say, less indiscipline 
in order to manage debt trajectories effectively, in particular in the countries which have high debt levels. And for instanc e, 
the Italian case is an interesting one because it is well known for its high debt level ratio. But in fact, it also has an 
advantageous position because it has no primary deficit. In fact, it even benefits from a large primary surplus.  
0:08:22 - Emmanuel Laborde 
As for the countries... For the other countries... I stop you because this will be a discussion with Guillaume Derrien during  
the next segment. Thank you very much, Hélène.

0:08:52 - Guillaume Derrien 
So, long-term rates have increased in all advanced economies, reaching levels not seen since 2008 in Europe and in the 
United States, and even since 1996 in Japan. Fortunately, the effective rate that states pay on their debt is lower than thos e

about? So, it's about 10 years in Japan, 13 years in the United Kingdom, and 8.5 years in France. That means that most of 
the debt was raised before rates started climbing. 
For example, in France, the effective rate just recently rose above the 2% mark, which is significantly lower than the 10 -
year bond yield, which exceeded 4% for new issuances.  
That said, in the United States, which has an average maturity of roughly six years, is experiencing a more rapid increase in

0:09:54 - Emmanuel Laborde
