0:10:05 - Guillaume Derrien 
Debt levels have reached unprecedented heights since the end of World War II. And at the same time, growth is lower, 
hovering around 2% on average in the United States, 1% in Europe and 0.5% in Japan. What does that mean for debt 
servicing costs? So, debt servicing cost is already relatively high, but it is expected to grow further as debt issue before the 
2022 rate hikes mature and are replaced with new instruments bearing higher interest rates. By 2030, interest costs could 
rise to levels last seen in 2000, specifically in France, around 4% of GDP. 
Are all the countries equally affected by these trends? Actually, no. Some countries are managing to reduce their debt 
level. 
Spain, Italy, even Japan have sufficiently improved their primary balance to achieve this, at least for the time being. And o f 
course, we must speak about Germany. Yes, Germany is implementing investment programs that result in a higher public 
deficit and debt-to-GDP ratio. But this ratio started from a relatively low base at around above 60% of GDP. That said, in 
this current context, German bonds have risen. And because German bonds are used as a benchmark for Europe, it  raises 
rates across all Eurozone countries. Is France the only country seeing its public debt go up?  
No, it's not, Emmanuel. This is happening in the United Kingdom and in the United States. These economies, like France, 
are running public deficits that are ever close or exceed 5% of GDP, which is too large for debt ratio to stabilize on its ow n. 
France and the United Kingdom are trying to gradually reduce their public deficit so as not to compromise growth. The 
United States is not going on that path yet, but market pressure could eventually force them to do so.  
0:12:08 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Thank you very much for your input, Guillaume. Thank you, Emmanuel. Let's go to the next segment. It's about the impact 
of rising bond yields on financing and investments in the Eurozone.  
0:12:26 - Thomas Humblot 
Hello, Thomas. Welcome. Hello. Thank you for having me today.  
0:12:30 - Emmanuel Laborde 
We've just seen what's behind the rise in Eurozone bond yields. This rise is partly attributed to the sovereign spread. What 
impact will this have on the economy? 
0:12:42 - Thomas Humblot 
Actually, this widening of some government bond spreads is obviously not good news, but it tends paradoxically to have 
less impact than an increase in long-term interest rates that does not include the sovereign risk premium. for example, in 
France, for a very long time, it has been considered that the 10 -year OAT was the relevant benchmark for fixed loans. And 
at that time, the spread on French government bonds was relatively stable. But since 2024, that spread increased

### Page 5

5

significantly and that renders the sovereign yield a less relevant benchmark.  
And now the most relevant benchmark is the swap rate because it determines the cost of bank funding that backs new 
loans. 
0:13:56 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Since the end of 2024, we have seen a rise in long-term 10-year swap rates. What impact does this have on bank lending 
rates? 
0:14:08 - Thomas Humblot 
Fixed loans have obviously become more expensive, but to a lesser extent than if they were priced solely based on long -
term sovereign rates. And what about existing fixed-rate loans? 
There is no impact. An increase in rates for the entire maturity of a loan does not have any effect if it is a fixed -rate loan. 
Borrowers with outstanding loans are therefore protected. Obviously, new fixed loans are granted at higher rates. And this 
sharply contrasts with a floating-rates loans. 
Because with floating-rates loans, borrowers experience regular rate adjustments throughout the term of the loan. And in 
terms of bank financing? Strictly in terms of bank financing, an increase in short -term rates has an immediate and 
widespread restrictive impact on borrowers, while for long-term interest rates, the impact is more gradual, and it comes 
gradually as the outstanding amount of fixed-rate loans are progressively renewed. And the thing is that since the 
beginning of the war in Iran, we have seen a combination of the two.  
And in practical terms, the rate of new bank loans to non-financial corporations has increased about 25 basis points in the 
eurozone since the beginning of the year. 
0:16:12 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:16:16 - Thomas Humblot

0:16:28 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:16:34 - Thomas Humblot

However, some significant bond issuance in the euro debt market due to some major US technology companies has 
increased spreads for longer maturities. 
0:17:11 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:17:19 - Thomas Humblot

and in turn that might lead to a decrease in demand for financing.

0:18:06 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Emerging bond markets held up well, which is quite unusual. The rise in long -term sovereign yields in emerging countries

markets over the summer, and the main currencies appreciated slightly against the dollar or the euro.  
0:18:37 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:18:43 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, it depends. You see, in cases like the Czech Republic and South Korea, public debt is moderate and macroeconomic 
fundamentals are solid. However, these countries are now less frequently considered as emerging and tend to be viewed 
as newly advanced economies. By contrast, Brazil and South Africa have weaker public finances and investors have 
already incorporated a higher sovereign risk into their assessments. And this is reflected in the bond yields. And this 
assessment of sovereign risk did not really change over the summer. 
0:19:26 - Emmanuel Laborde 
When comparing the main fiscal metrics, what are the trends? What do you see as an economist?  
0:19:33 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
On average, the fiscal metrics in emerging markets are not substantially worse than those in advanced economies. The 
average budget deficit levels are similar, ranging from 4% to 5% of GDP in 2025 -2026, according to the IMF. And as in 
advanced economies, the public debt is higher than its pre-Covid level. However, the average public debt to GDP ratio in 
emerging countries excluding China is close to 60%, whereas in advanced economies, it is above 100%.  
0:20:12 - Emmanuel Laborde 
Do you arrive at the same conclusion when comparing debt sustainability criteria?  
0:20:17 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, there are significant regional differences. In emerging Asia, the factors that determine debt sustainability are more 
often favorable than those in advanced economies. In Central Europe, the gap between the interest rate paid on debt and 
the GDP growth rate is also favorable. However, the primary deficits in some countries are high.  
0:20:45 - Emmanuel Laborde

0:20:49 - Cynthia Kalasopatan-Antoine 
Well, Brazil and Mexico are in a less favorable position. These countries would indeed need to generate a significant 
primary budget surplus each year in order to stabilize their debt in the medium term.

0:22:58 - Isabelle Mateos y Lago

rates, in some cases, rising them further. And the responsibility for keeping public finances on a sustainable footing rests

banks to lower interest rates prematurely would be enormously counterproductive.

這次我們想做個新嘗試。
之前幾期的「財報解剖」⼀向以極致的深度著稱，但⽼實說，並⾮每次財報公
佈，都有必要把所有維度拉出來重新解構⼀遍。
有些時候，最關鍵的變化其實只集中在兩三個核⼼變數上。
因此，我們推出了這個更聚焦、節奏更快的變體版。

雖然篇幅縮減，但我們對商業本質的思考沒有打折。這裡保留了最精華的洞
察，保證依然⽐市⾯上絕⼤多數的財報分析更有⾒解。
這份分析由我們的分析師  Benita 主導撰寫，我負責校正觀點並做最後把關，希
望能⽤更⾼效的⽅式，幫⼤家抓出最值得關注的產業訊號。
Palantir ⼜⼀次完美財報，企業 AI
開始真正變現，但新簽訂單卻放緩了？

### Page 2

Palantir 第三季⼜交出了⼀份近乎沒有瑕疵的財報。
2026 年第三季營收來到  23.13 億美元，年增  96% 、季增  20% ，創下公司史上
最⾼的年增率，同時也是連續第  13 個季度營收成⻑加速。

### Page 3

其中美國商業營收⼤增  148% ，調整後營業利益率從去年同期的  51% 躍升⾄
62% ，推升  Rule of 40 達到  158% 。
同時，公司還把第四季營收指引調⾼到約  26.51 億美元，⾼於市場原本預期的
約  24.7 億美元（⾼出約  7% ）。
只要第四季順利達成指引， Palantir 的年化營收規模就將正式跨過  100 億美元
⼤關。
不過， Palantir 的「好」，已經不是新聞了。
我們會將這次的分析分為 4 個重點。
重點⼀：企業 AI 開始真正變現 —— 從百萬美元試點，⾛向千萬美
元的營運系統
過去這兩年，許多企業在導⼊ AI 時，最常⾒的做法就是去買⼤模型的 API 、做
個內部聊天機器⼈，或是花錢做⼀堆概念驗證（ PoC ）。
但試了⼀圈之後，許多企業開始感到挫折。
⼀⽅⾯，對外串接純⼤模型供應商，很難完全保護公司內部的機密資料與營運
知識；另⼀⽅⾯，讓 AI 寫寫信、做個摘要並不難，真正的挑戰在於如何讓 AI
融⼊公司的⽇常⼯作流程，並創造出⾜以回本的經濟效益。

為了解決這個痛點， Palantir 過去透過實戰⼯作坊（ AIP Bootcamp ，近期已進⼀
步演進為打造 AI 代理的 AgentCamp ），讓企業帶著⾃⼰的真實資料，在短時間
內做出⼀套能實際運作的系統。
到了今年，這套模式更延伸到「主權 AI （ Sovereign AI ）」。
這裡談的 AI 主權，重點在於讓企業⾃⼰掌握資料、業務邏輯與模型選擇權。 企
業可以⾃由更換底層模型，並把真正值錢的營運知識留在⾃⼰的系統裡。
在這個架構下，企業可以利⽤⾃⼰累積的營運資料，在 Palantir 的 Ontology （本
體論）架構下評估並微調開放權重模型，完全不必每件事都去呼叫最昂貴的頂
級⼤模型。
在這次法說會上，有兩個案例特別有說服⼒：
第⼀個是國防科技公司 L3Harris 。

### Page 4

他們利⽤⾃家資料微調開放模型後，在不到 48 ⼩時內，就讓這套主權模型的表
現超越了原本使⽤的前沿⼤模型，⽽且初步結果顯⽰整體成本⼤幅降低了
95% 。
第⼆個案例則是連 NVIDIA ⾃⼰都在⽤。
NVIDIA 透過 Palantir 評估並重新訓練（ post-training ）⾃家的開放模型，結果成
功讓旗下體積最⼩、效率最⾼的模型，在表現上⼤幅超越了⾃家最⼤的模型。
企業在⽇常營運中，往往更需要⼀個真正懂內部業務、且成本合理的專屬
模型，勝過⼀味追求最昂貴卻不懂公司流程的通⽤⼤模型。
舉個簡單的例⼦，⼀家製造商如果要⽤ AI 判斷哪些零件該優先採購，它真正需
要的是模型能讀懂公司的供應商名單、⽣產排程、庫存限制和交貨承諾，並根
據最新狀況隨時調整。
Palantir 想掌握的，正是把底層模型轉化為實際⼯作流程的這⼀層價值。
當企業發現這套做法真的能解決問題，從試點轉為正式⼤單的速度就相當快。

### Page 5

這次財報中， Palantir 公布了四個在⼀兩季內從⼩型試點迅速擴張成⼤型合約的
案例，擴張後的年化合約價值（ ACV ）合計達到 7,650 萬美元：
家電製造商： 從 100 萬美元的初始試點，擴張⾄ 1,500 萬美元的年化合
約。
⼯業材料公司： 從近 300 萬美元試點，擴張⾄ 2,900 萬美元。
全球運動品牌： 從 160 萬美元試點，擴張⾄約 2,100 萬美元。
服飾企業： 透過 AgentCamp 啟動 250 萬美元試點，隨後擴張⾄ 1,150 萬美
元。
其中，那家家電製造商的變化特別有代表性。
他們在第⼆季才剛花 100 萬美元試⽔溫，很快就轉成 300 萬美元的合約；結果
到了第三季，合約⾦額⼜⼀⼝氣放⼤五倍來到 1,500 萬美元，使⽤範圍直接橫
跨銷售（商務）、財務與供應鏈三⼤部⾨。

### Page 6

這代表客戶⼀旦把單⼀功能跑順了，就會開始把不同部⾨的⼯作流程全部串進
同⼀個平台，預算規模也跟著翻倍跳升。
重點⼆：美國商業營收⾸度超⾞政府 —— ⽼客戶越買越多，撐起
第⼆顆成⻑引擎
當單⼀客戶的合約⾦額從⼀百萬變成上千萬美元，反映在全公司的總體財報
上，就促成了這次最具指標性的轉折：
Palantir 的美國商業營收，歷史上第⼀次超越了美國政府營收。
我們可以看看第三季美國市場這兩⼤業務的表現：
美國商業客戶營收：達到  9.84 億美元，年增  148%（季增  29% ）。
美國政府客戶營收：達到  9.53 億美元，年增  96% 。

### Page 7

如果把海外市場也加進來，整體商業總營收來到  11.7 億美元，同樣在歷史上⾸
度超越整體政府營收的  11.5 億美元。
⽐起破紀錄的成⻑率，營收組合的翻轉其實更具代表性。
過去市場對  Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商；但
隨著商業營收全⾯超⾞，華爾街在它⾝上套了⼗年的「政府承包商」標籤，在
這⼀季正式失效。
過去市場對 Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商。這類
業務雖然⾨檻極⾼，但容易受到政府預算審查與採購週期的影響。
值得注意的是，這次商業營收能夠超⾞，背後依然伴隨著極為強勁的政府業
務。
美國政府營收年增 96% ，幾乎翻倍；純粹是因為美國企業客戶導⼊的速度衝得
更快，讓公司成功⻑出了第⼆顆規模相當、⽽且爆發⼒更強的成⻑引擎。
如果再往下⼀層看，會有⼀個⽐營收交叉更有意思的發現。

### Page 8

第三季美國商業客戶的總數來到 686 家，年增 29% ；但同期美國商業營收卻⼤
增了 148% 。為什麼營收成⻑的速度，會遠遠甩開客戶數量增加的速度？
答案就在於「淨收⼊留存率（ NDR ）」。這⼀季 Palantir 的整體淨收⼊留存率，
從上⼀季的 157% 進⼀步攀升到 165% 。
這說明了推動營收翻倍的主⼒，很⼤部分來⾃既有客戶持續加碼。
就像前⾯提到的製造商⼀樣，⼀家企業可能先⽤ Palantir 管庫存，覺得好⽤之
後，接著把採購、排程、財務到 AI 決策全部放進來。
不過，這波企業 AI 變現潮⽬前還是⾼度集中在美國。

### Page 9

相較於美國商業營收 148% 的⾼成⻑，第三季國際商業營收只有 1.84 億美元，
年增 21% ，季增更只有 1% 。
這意味著美國企業對軟體轉型的接受度依然領先全球，⽽未來能否把美國驗證
成功的模式複製到海外市場，將是後續觀察的重點。
重點三：營收翻倍但員⼯數幾乎沒變 —— ⾼營業槓桿背後的結構
與代價
把前⾯兩件事串起來看：當客戶透過⼯作坊體驗產品後迅速擴⼤採⽤，⽽且導
⼊之後⼜會⾃⼰越買越多（ NDR 165% ），這會對 Palantir 的成本結構帶來什麼
影響？
執⾏⻑ Alex Karp 在接受媒體（ MarketWatch ）訪問時特別提到了⼀個營運細
節：
Palantir 在美國商業營收⼤增 148% 的同時，全公司的員⼯總數（ Headcount ）
幾乎維持持平。

### Page 10

在傳統的企業軟體產業裡，公司如果想把營收規模擴⼤⼀倍，通常得按⽐例招
募⼤量的業務代表、售前顧問與⽀援⼯程師，⼈⼒成本也會跟著⽔漲船⾼。
但 Karp 形容 Palantir 的成⻑基本上不太依賴傳統意義上的業務員。對他們來說，
產品本⾝（ Foundry 、 Ontology 加上 AIP ）在⼯作坊裡的實際表現，就是最好的
銷售⼯具。
更重要的是，因為底層的資料整合與基礎架構在初期導⼊時就已經建好了，當
客戶後續決定把合約從 300 萬美元擴⼤到 1,500 萬美元時， Palantir 並不需要再投
⼊同等⽐例的⼯程⼈⼒從頭開發。
當營收以接近翻倍的速度成⻑，交付這些服務所需的總⼈數卻沒有等⽐例
增加，這展現了軟體業相當強勁的營業槓桿。
這項特質解釋了為什麼 Palantir 能在⾼成⻑的同時，交出亮眼的獲利數字：
第三季 GAAP 營業利益率達到 47% ，調整後（ Non-GAAP ）營業利益率⾼達
62% ，進⽽推升 Rule of 40 來到 158% 的⽔準。
不過，這種⾼營業槓桿背後也有相對應的代價。

雖然公司的總員⼯數幾乎持平，但為了留住頂尖⼈才並獎勵績效，第三季的股
權激勵費⽤（ SBC ）從去年同期的 1.72 億美元⼤幅增加到 3.19 億美元，幾乎翻
了⼀倍。
這也正是  GAAP 營業利益率（ 47% ）與調整後營業利益率（ 62% ）之間相差 15
個百分點的主要原因。
重點四：拆解 TCV 放緩與 RPO 持平的真相

### Page 11

這份完美財報裡唯⼀的裂縫，在於反映未來幾年營收可⾒度的單季「新簽總合
約價值（ TCV ）」與「未履約義務（ RPO ）」出現了明顯放緩：
整體  TCV ：第三季為  29.18 億美元，年增率僅  6% （相⽐第⼆季為年增
49% ），⽽且較上⼀季的  33.73 億美元下滑了  13.5% 。
美國商業  TCV ：第三季為  14.63 億美元，年增率降⾄  11% （相⽐第⼆季為
年增  153% ），為  2023 年以來的最低單季增速。
剩餘交易價值（ RDV ）與未履約義務（ RPO ）：單季來看，整體  RDV 季
增僅  2% （美國商業  RDV 季增  4% ，低於上⼀季的  27% ）， RPO 則幾乎持
平。
當季營收⼤增  96% 、新簽訂單卻只成⻑  6%，這兩個數字在財務邏輯上不可能
⻑期並存。
當季營收主要是由既有客戶擴張所推動，⽽新簽約指標的放緩，⾃然讓部分投
資⼈開始擔⼼未來幾年的營收可⾒度。
針對這個現象，財務⻑ David Glazer 與經營團隊在法說會上的解釋是：

### Page 12

許多客戶為了快速導⼊ Sovereign AI 的初期功能、急著讓系統趕快上線，所以
在第三季簽下了好幾筆千萬美元級別、但「合約期限⽐往常更短」的訂單。
管理層同時強調，「 Sovereign AI ⾰命才剛滿⼀季」，並預期隨著客戶使⽤深
⼊，這些合約的年限與規模都會隨時間增加。
管理層的這番解釋，其實也剛好從會計規則上說明了為什麼 RPO 幾乎持平：因
為 Palantir 的 RPO 定義，本來就不計⼊初始期限少於 12 個⽉的短期合約。
如果第三季新簽的  Sovereign AI ⼤單多半屬於⼀年以內的短約，那麼不會反映
在 RPO 上、同時壓低單季 TCV 總額，確實符合財務邏輯。
此外，如果我們把時間拉⻑來看，⻑約存量指標其實沒有單季數字看起來那麼
疲弱：
整體  RDV （剩餘交易價值）： 年增率仍達  55% 。
美國商業  RDV ： 達到  65.2 億美元，年增率⾼達  79% 。
過去  12 個⽉（ TTM ）美國商業  TCV ： 累計達  61.14 億美元，年增

62% 。
換句話說，單季簽約指標放緩，還不等於企業端的需求轉弱。真正的問題在於
估值給出的容錯空間極⼩。
以約  178 美元的股價與  24.03 億股計算， Palantir 的市值約  4,286 億美元；扣除
帳上  104 億美元的現⾦與美國公債後，企業價值（ EV ）約為  4,182 億美元。
對照全年營收指引中值  85.32 億美元，企業價值倍數（ EV/Sales ）⾼達  49 倍；
就算⽤第四季指引年化的  106 億美元來算，也⾼達約  39 倍。
將近  40 到  50 倍的營收定價，隱含市場要求這家公司在跨過百億美元年化營收
之後，未來⼗年還得維持約  50% 以上的年複合成⻑率。
在這麼⾼的⾨檻下，市場沒有太多耐⼼等待。
下⼀季投資⼈最想驗證的，就是這批短約能不能如管理層所說，順利續約成更
⻑、更⼤的多年期合約。
從更⼤的產業趨勢來看，企業 AI 的投資重⼼正在轉移。
第⼀波浪潮，⼤家搶著買 GPU 、買算⼒、買⼤模型 API ；但進⼊第⼆階段，企
業真正關⼼的是如何結合內部資料與⼯作流程，讓 AI 實際改善營運並省下成

本。
Palantir 已經在美國市場證明⾃⼰能吃到這波轉型的紅利。

### Page 13

接下來決定它⻑期價值的關鍵，就在於這批新導⼊的短期合約能否在未來幾季
轉化為⻑約存量，並將這套商業模式進⼀步複製到更廣⼤的海外市場。
蘋果：營收創紀錄、⽑利被記憶體成本
壓縮， Service 令⼈失望？
Apple 這次財報，表⾯上相當不錯。
第四季營收來到 1,145 億美元，年增 12% ；每股盈餘 2.10 美元，年增 14% 。主
⼒產品 iPhone 營收成⻑ 17% ， Mac 更是⼤幅成⻑ 24% 。

### Page 14

但在這些亮眼數字背後，如果仔細拆解財報細節與管理層在法說會上的說法，
會發現幾個值得留意的變化。
⾸先是⽑利率。
這⼀季表⾯上 47.9% 的⽑利率，其實包含了約 1 個百分點的關稅退款⼀次性收
益（每股盈餘 2.10 美元裡，也有 0.05 美元來⾃同⼀筆退款）。

### Page 15

扣掉這筆⼀次性收益後，本業⽑利率⼤約是 46.9% ；但就算⽤這個基準來⽐，
Apple 給出的下⼀季（ 12 ⽉季度）⽑利率指引落在 45% ⾄ 46% ，依然在往下⾛。
要知道， 12 ⽉季度是 Apple 傳統的銷售最旺季，營收規模放⼤通常會推升⽑利
率，為什麼這次反⽽逆勢下滑？
財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 在法說會上給出了答案：下⼀季⽑利率下滑，「主要」反映
記憶體成本上升，「另外也包括 Siri AI 上線的部分成本」。
這句話其實就是貫穿整份財報的核⼼線索：全球 AI 浪潮正同時從「硬體成
本」與「軟體商業模式」兩端夾擊 Apple 。
AI 確實帶來了新的成⻑機會，但也正在改變 Apple 原本最賺錢的獲利結構。
我們可以把這次財報拆解成五個環環相扣的重點來看：
重點⼀：硬體的成本逆⾵ —— 全球 AI 算⼒戰，為什麼由 Apple
先買單？

### Page 16

下⼀季⽑利率下滑的最⼤推⼿，來⾃記憶體成本的飆升。 這背後反映了⼀個很
現實的產業現象： Apple 雖然想靠 AI 賺錢，但現在卻得先為全球的 AI 資料中⼼
基建買單。
CEO John Ternus 在法說會上直接⽤「前所未⾒（ unprecedented ）」來形容當
前的記憶體價格環境。
其實在 9 ⽉這⼀季， Apple 就已經感受到記憶體漲價的壓⼒，只是當時⼿裡還有
早先囤積的低價庫存，加上其他⾮記憶體零組件成本下降，才抵銷掉部分衝
擊。
但隨著低價庫存逐漸消耗，這股成本壓⼒將在 12 ⽉季度正式浮現；管理層也坦
⾔，在 12 ⽉季度之後，記憶體採購價格預計仍會持續上升，⽽研調機構更普遍
預期這波供應緊俏會⼀路延續到 2027 年。
為了應對這場結構性的缺貨， Apple 已經採取了兩個動作：
直接漲價： 6 ⽉底先調漲了 Mac 與 iPad 的價格， 9 ⽉⼜將 iPhone 售價調⾼
約 100 美元，且管理層並未排除未來進⼀步漲價的可能。

### Page 17

砸錢鎖定產能： Apple 的庫存⾦額單季⼤增 31% 、年增⾼達 154% ，來到約
145 億美元；從 6 ⽉到 9 ⽉季度，製造採購承諾⾦額更是⼀⼝氣暴增了 330
億美元。
憑藉龐⼤的現⾦流與規模， Apple 搶貨的能⼒絕對⽐中⼩型 Android ⼿機廠或 PC
廠強得多。
但記憶體終究是每⼀台硬體裝置中無法妥協的核⼼成本，當漲價轉嫁給消費者
到了⼀定極限、開始有拉⻑換機週期的⾵險時，硬體⽑利率勢必得承受壓⼒。
全球掀起 AI 資料中⼼擴建潮，最先賺到錢的是記憶體⼤廠，⽽包括 Apple
在內的終端硬體商，正在為這場硬體通膨買單。
重點⼆：⾼⽑利新武器卡關 ——iPhone Duo 的產能與初期品管
挑戰

### Page 18

既然記憶體漲價壓縮了硬體⽑利，最⾃然的解法，就是多賣⼀點⾼單價、⾼⽑
利的新產品來拉⾼平均售價（ ASP ）。
這就帶出了市場最關注的新品： Apple ⾸款折疊機 iPhone Duo 。
iPhone Duo 在 9 ⽉底財報結算後才正式上市，因此第四季財報還沒有反映它的
營收。
從需求⾯來看，這款新機⾮常成功，預購迅速秒殺，出貨時間⼀路排到明年⼀
⽉下旬。
但問題出在供給⾯。
這次法說會上， CEO John Ternus 特別安排營運⻑ Sabih Khan ⼀起上線回答供應
鏈問題。
Khan 坦⾔， iPhone Duo ⽬前處於「產能受限」的狀態，且「很難預測供需何
時能達到平衡」。

### Page 19

這種罕⾒不給時間表的說法，意味著折疊螢幕與轉軸的量產爬坡難度，⽐過去
傳統 iPhone ⾼出許多。
更⿇煩的是，初期有極少數⽤戶反映內螢幕轉軸處出現摺痕或裂紋的品質問
題；雖然 Apple 已經在協助換機，但緊繃的產能也讓部分地區的換貨等待時間
拉⻑。
除了折疊機，過去受限於先進製程（隨著產品線轉向 2 奈⽶已逐漸緩解）的
Mac 與 iPad ，到了 12 ⽉季度依然⾯臨缺貨，⽽這次卡住產能的瓶頸，剛好⼜是
前⾯提到的記憶體不⾜。
換句話說，儘管下⼀季營收預計仍能成⻑ 7% ⾄ 10% 、有望創下公司歷史新
⾼，但記憶體漲價加上⾼階新品缺貨，讓硬體業務在短期內很難幫公司撐起⽑
利率。
這時候， Apple 就更需要仰賴⾼⽑利的 Services （服務業務）來穩住獲利基本
盤。
第四季 Services 營收只占公司總營收約 28% ，卻因為⾼達 76% 的⽑利率，貢獻
了全公司將近 44% 的⽑利。

### Page 20

硬體負責把⼈帶進⽣態系， Services 負責賺取⾼利潤，這是 Apple 最核⼼的賺錢
公式。
然⽽，正如財務⻑在⽑利率指引中透露的，連 Services 這座⾼⽑利堡壘，也從
下⼀季開始要⾯對 AI 帶來的成本考驗。
重點三： Siri AI 終於有了收費⽅向，但會不會稀釋 76% 的⾼⽑
利？
Apple 在今年 WWDC 就已經宣布，部分需要雲端模型處理的 AI 功能將設有使
⽤額度，使⽤者未來可以付費取得更多運算容量。
這次法說會上， Goldman Sachs 分析師 Michael Ng 進⼀步追問： iCloud+ 是否正
在轉型成 Apple AI 商業化的重要⼯具？
管理層給出了肯定的答案，確認未來需要更多伺服器端 AI 運算能⼒的使⽤者，
可以付費購買額外容量。
過去⼤家訂閱 iCloud+ ，買的是「儲存空間」，⽤來放照⽚、影⽚和備份檔案。
到了 AI 時代，使⽤者額外付費購買的變成了「運算能⼒」，像是讓 Siri 分析複
雜⽂件、執⾏多次雲端請求，或是呼叫⼤型模型處理⼯作。

### Page 21

坐擁超過 25 億台活躍裝置、超過 15 億個付費訂閱的 Apple ，要推這項新服務當
然很有優勢。
假設未來 Apple 能吸引 5,000 萬⼈、每⽉額外付 10 美元使⽤進階 AI 功能，⼀年
就能帶來 60 億美元的營收，相當於⽬前全年 Services 營收的 4.9% 左右。
但從財務⾓度來看，這⾨⽣意跟過去的數位服務有⼀個本質上的差異。
傳統數位服務的營收增加，不太需要同⽐例增加成本；但⼤型 AI 模型每次執⾏
雲端推論，背後都在消耗真實的算⼒資源。使⽤者越頻繁使⽤複雜的 AI 功能，
Apple 要承擔的伺服器與推論成本就越⾼。

### Page 22

過去每收到 100 美元服務收⼊， Apple 可以留下 76 美元⽑利；但到了 AI 服務
時代，營收增加的同時，背後往往伴隨著持續攀升的雲端推論成本。
這也解釋了為什麼財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 特別強調， Siri AI 的雲端推論費⽤將
計⼊ Services 的銷貨成本，⽽且相關成本已經開始反映在 12 ⽉季度的⽑利率指
引中。
同時， Apple 還透露了⼀個會計處理上的改變：當 Siri AI 成為硬體裝置隨附的服
務時，公司會將⼀部分的硬體收⼊分配給這項服務，並在裝置使⽤期間逐步認
列。
這代表就算消費者沒有額外掏錢加購算⼒， Apple 的 Services 營收與成本結構，
也會因為 AI 功能的推出⽽重新洗牌。
因此，接下來觀察 Apple 的 AI 變現能⼒，除了看有多少⼈願意付費，更要看每
增加⼀美元的 AI 收⼊，究竟能留下多少⽑利。
這也是為什麼 Apple 持續投⼊⾃研晶⽚與裝置端 AI ，除了讓⼿機跑得更順，
背後更有明確的財務考量：把越多的運算留在裝置端處理，⻑期下來就能

省下越多的雲端推論成本。

### Page 23

重點四：華爾街真正擔⼼的事 —— 當 AI Agent 成為⼊⼝，
Apple 還能收多少過路費？
如果說推論成本影響的是「利潤率」，那 Morgan Stanley 和 Evercore 分析師在
法說會上追問的，則是關係到「營收來源」的根本問題：
當 AI 從聊天機器⼈演變成能替使⽤者執⾏任務的 Agent （代理），會不會威脅
到 Apple 現有的 Services 業務？
我們可以想像⼀個很⽇常的場景。
現在如果你想規劃⼀趟旅⾏，通常會先打開 Google 搜尋機票，進到航空公司網
站⽐價，接著打開訂房 App 找飯店，最後分別完成付款。整個過程必須切換好
幾個不同的平台與 App 。
但在不久的將來，你可能只需要對⼿機裡的 AI Agent 說⼀句：「幫我安排下週末
去東京的旅⾏，機票加飯店預算三萬台幣，飯店希望離⾞站近⼀點。」
接下來， AI Agent 就會⾃動幫你搜尋、篩選，並直接串接不同服務完成預訂。
從頭到尾，你可能連訂房 App 都沒有打開過。
這個轉變最核⼼的影響，在於「誰掌握了決定權」。 過去是使⽤者決定要打開

哪個 App ；未來則是 AI Agent 決定要調⽤哪⼀家服務商。

### Page 24

⼀旦消費者習慣把⽇常決策交給 ChatGPT 、 Gemini 或其他第三⽅ AI Agent ，
Apple 過去靠 iOS 與 App Store 築起的收費關卡就會⾯臨兩個挑戰：
App Store 分潤壓⼒： 如果第三⽅  Agent 直接透過網⾴、外部  API 或其他
⽀付管道完成交易， Apple 對數位商品與訂閱交易的抽成能⼒就會被繞
過。
搜尋授權收⼊重分配： Apple 每年從搜尋引擎拿到的巨額授權⾦，建⽴在
「 iPhone 是最重要搜尋⼊⼝」的商業價值上。當  AI Agent 逐漸取代傳統
搜尋與  App 操作，這個⼊⼝的經濟價值勢必會重新洗牌。

### Page 25

⾯對這個問題， John Ternus 的回答展現了 Apple 的防守策略。
他強調， iPhone 是使⽤者最重要的個⼈資訊中⼼，裡⾯存放著聯絡⼈、訊息、
照⽚、⾏事曆、健康與財務資料。
AI 如果想真正理解使⽤者需求並跨 App 完成任務，就必須取得這些個⼈脈絡與
系統權限。
這讓未來的 AI Agent 競爭，分成了兩種不同的控制權：
需求⼊⼝： 誰最懂使⽤者想做什麼，誰來決定推薦哪些商品與服務（這
是  OpenAI 、 Google 正在搶的）。
執⾏⼊⼝： 誰能安全地讀取個⼈資料、呼叫  App 功能，並完成最後的操
作（這是  Apple 透過  iOS 、 App Intents 與  Siri AI 牢牢握在⼿裡的）。

### Page 26

Apple 很清楚⾃⼰未必要做出全世界最龐⼤的模型，但它必須利⽤作業系統與
隱私權限的優勢，把「需求」與「執⾏」整合在⾃⼰的平台上，確保使⽤者依
然透過 Siri 來完成⽇常任務。
⽽這場⼊⼝保衛戰之所以迫在眉睫，從這次 Services 的財測就能看出端倪。
Apple 第四季 Services 營收達到創紀錄的 315.7 億美元，年增約 10% ；但下⼀季
的成⻑指引卻放緩到 8% ⾄ 9% 。
管理層解釋，放緩的主因來⾃「第三⽅授權收⼊」與「匯率」變化，其次則是
部分 App Store 與⼿遊業務的調整。

### Page 27

值得注意的是，管理層⼝中的「第三⽅授權收⼊」，背後很⼤⼀部分就是
Google ⽀付給 Apple 的搜尋引擎分潤。
雖然單季度的波動未必完全來⾃ AI Agent 的衝擊，但這正好印證了華爾街的擔
憂：只要搜尋授權與 App Store 這兩⼤⽀柱稍微放緩， Services 的成⻑動能就會
⽴刻感受到壓⼒。
重點五：現在的股價，到底在賭什麼？
把前⾯硬體與軟體的拼圖全部放在⼀起，我們最後來看估值。
以財報當⽇收盤價約 338 美元、以及 FY2026 實際每股盈餘 8.98 美元計算，
Apple 的歷史本益⽐約為 37.7 倍（財報公布後盤後股價回落⾄約 329 美元，換算
也⾼達 36.6 倍）。

### Page 28

37.7 倍的本益⽐聽起來很貴，但「貴」是⼀個很模糊的詞。換個問法會清楚很
多：如果今天⽤ 338 美元買進 Apple ，然後⼀直抱著不賣，你每年⼤概能賺多
少？
有⼀個很簡單的算法：
⻑期年報酬  ≈ 現⾦殖利率  + ⻑期成⻑率
現⾦殖利率，就是公司⼀年賺進的⾃由現⾦流，除以你付出的市值。
Apple 現在市值接近 5 兆美元，⼀年的⾃由現⾦流⼤約只有市值的 2.6% 。
換句話說，⽤ 338 美元買進，第⼀年真正拿回的現⾦回報只有 2.6% 左右，剩下
的報酬全靠未來成⻑。
⽤這個邏輯反推，可以從兩個⾓度看現在的股價：
如果假設  Apple ⻑期每年成⻑  3% ：
338 美元的股價，換算下來的⻑期年報酬⼤約是  6.1% （註：若以純現⾦殖利率
2.6% 直接相加則約為  5.6% ）。

### Page 29

同⼀時間，美國⼗年期公債殖利率約  4.9% ，⽽且幾乎沒有⾵險。
這代表買  Apple ⽐買公債多承擔了股價波動的⾵險，每年卻只多換來約  1 個百
分點左右的⾵險補償；⽽在歷史上，美股投資⼈要求的⾵險補償通常落在  4%
到  5% 。
如果投資⼈要求每年  7% 的報酬：
這只⽐公債多  2 個百分點左右，已經算很客氣了。
但要做到這樣， Apple 必須永遠每年成⻑約  3.6% 以上。
⼀般估值模型給成熟公司的永續成⻑率，多半抓在  2% 到  3% ；要求  3.6% ，等
於要求⼀家市值近  5 兆美元的巨頭，永遠跟著整個美國經濟的速度⼀起⻑⼤。
這兩個⾓度講的是同⼀件事：現在的股價，幾乎沒有留空間給意外。
這時候再回頭看⽑利率，就能理解市場為什麼對那 1 到 2 個百分點的變化這麼
敏感。

### Page 30

以 2026 財年全年 Services ⾼達 1,233 億美元的營收規模來算，服務⽑利率只要
每下降 1 個百分點，⼀年就少掉約 12.3 億美元的⽑利。
若放⼤到全公司約 4,790 億美元的總營收來看，全公司⽑利率每少 1 個百分點，
扣稅後⼤約會少掉每股 0.27 美元的盈餘；⽤ 37.7 倍的本益⽐換算，等於股價直
接少了約 10 美元。
更別忘了，連計算 37.7 倍本益⽐所⽤的 8.98 美元 EPS 起點，裡⾯都還包含了⼀
筆關稅退款的⼀次性收益。
所以這次財報最值得思考的地⽅，在於現在的股價，其實已經先預設了 Apple
會有個完美結局：
市場預設記憶體漲價很快會過去、 Siri AI 的雲端算⼒成本控制得住，⽽且第三
⽅ AI Agent 也搶不⾛ iPhone 的流量⼊⼝。
對 Apple 來說，投⼊ AI 同時是⼀項成⻑投資，也是⼀筆為了保護每年 1,233 億
美元服務⽣意的必要⽀出。
只要硬體缺料漲價、 AI 推論成本攀升、或是⼊⼝分流這三件事當中有任何⼀件

拖得⽐預期更久，市場除了要下修獲利預估，連那 37.7 倍的估值倍數本⾝都可
能⾯臨重新定價。
⽽在現在這個價位，後者帶來的影響往往更⼤。

Transformation 
Quantum dictionary, part 2: From potential to progress 
02 November 2026

Key takeaways 
• In the second and final part of our quantum dictionary series, we move beyond the basics to explore where quantum could go 
next. Today's quantum computers remain limited by noise and errors, but their potential grows rapidly as they scale. Emerging 
applications like quantum AI and sensing could eventually unlock new capabilities and significant economic value. 
• Quantum communication could change both how we connect quantum computers and how we protect information. A quantum 
internet could use entanglement to link quantum systems, creating additional computing power, while quantum networks could 
make eavesdropping detectable and offer new ways to securely transmit information. 
• Meanwhile, the race for quantum leadership is already underway. Governments are pursuing greater quantum sovereignty as the 
technology's potential implications for competitiveness, security and defense grow.

Assessing quantum’s performance and progress 
Quantum advantage versus supremacy 
Quantum advantage is reached when a quantum computer can solve a useful, real-world problem faster than a classical 
computer. The problem is, today’s quantum computers are still too noisy, as we discussed in part one of this series, and qubit 
quality isn’t yet high enough to demonstrate quantum advantage.  
Quantum supremacy takes this one step further. It refers to the point when a quantum computer has computational advantage, 
meaning it can solve a problem that a traditional computer couldn’t in any feasible amount of time.  
Quantum scaling 
Quantum scaling is about building bigger, more powerful quantum computers. It involves overcoming the challenges of 
increasing both the number and the quality of qubits. Today, we’re in the noisy intermediate-scale quantum era. Quantum 
computers have tens to a few hundred qubits, but noise and errors still limit what they can do.1 
However, when quantum computers do scale, they do so exponentially. Every additional qubit doubles the number of operations 
the system can perform. A quantum computer with just 10 qubits could perform more than 100 times the operations of a 
classical computer (Exhibit 1). More qubits could therefore mean more operations and, ultimately, more economic value in the 
industries where quantum computing can be put to work, according to BofA Global Research. 
Exhibit 1: A quantum computer with just 10 qubits could perform more than 100 times the operations than classical 
Number of classical versus quantum operations for a given (qu)bit number (log scale)

Quantum Benchmarking Initiative 
The Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency’s (DARPA’s) Quantum Benchmarking Initiative (QBI) aims to answer a big 
question: Is it possible to build a commercially relevant quantum computer by 2033? To be clear, QBI is not a competition 
between companies. DARPA evaluates all viable approaches for which funding is available.  
QBI has three stages:  
• Stage A: Start with a concept. At this stage, there is a utility-scale quantum computer concept that has a plausible path to 
realization in the near term. 
• Stage B: Put a plan behind it. This stage involves developing a research and development plan, identifying the associated 
risks and introducing prototypes to overcome them. 
• Stage C: Put it to the test. In the final stage, companies work with the government to verify and validate that the utility-
scale quantum computer concept can be built as intended. 
Exploring emerging quantum applications  
Quantum AI 
According to BofA Global Research, quantum AI brings together two powerful technologies: AI and quantum computing, with the 
potential to improve both the physical and the digital worlds. And the relationship could work both ways. Quantum computing 
could provide some of the compute power needed to train generative AI, while generative AI could help speed up the 
development and testing time of quantum systems.  
AI tools like machine learning (ML) could also make quantum error correction (QEC) more efficient and scalable.2 ML models 
trained on simulated datasets can identify and correct errors faster than traditional methods while using fewer computing 
resources. They can also more accurately predict errors before they happen.3 
Quantum sensing 
Quantum sensing uses the properties of quantum mechanics to measure things like magnetic fields, gravitational forces, time 
and rotation with greater precision than classical instruments (Exhibit 2).4 
Exhibit 2: Quantum sensors use quantum properties to measure physical quantities with unmatched precision 
An infographic of quantum sensing

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

3 Swayne, M. (2025, January 6). AI for Quantum Error Correction: A Comprehensive Guide to Using Artificial Intelligence to Improve Quantum Error Correction. 
Quantum Insider.  
4 Rehman, M.U. (2026, March 2). Understanding Quantum Sensing and Its Industrial Potential. Quantum Insider.

### Page 3

02 November 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

So, how does it work? In simple terms, there are three steps. First, a quantum system is prepared in a well-defined initial state, 
for example, by cooling an atom to near absolute zero. Next, the sensor is exposed to whatever is being measured, causing a 
subtle shift in its quantum state. In an atomic magnetometer, for example, a magnetic field changes the energy levels of an 
atom’s electrons, which changes how the atom absorbs or emits light. Finally, the sensor measures how much the quantum state 
has shifted, revealing the strength of the external force.5  
Benefits and challenges 
Compared with quantum computers, quantum sensors face some lower technical barriers. They don’t need nearly as many qubits 
to be useful, entanglement can help but isn’t required for every application, and even short coherence times can produce useful 
measurements.6 The catch? They often require large vacuum chambers, laser systems and magnetic shielding to maintain 
coherence. 
The quantum internet and communications  
Quantum communication 
Quantum communication is the study of how information can be encoded and transmitted between quantum systems over a 
distance (Exhibit 3). It uses properties like superposition and entanglement to enable the secure transmission of information. 
Why does that matter? One of the main goals is to keep information secure from potential eavesdroppers, particularly as more 
powerful quantum computers could pose new challenges for traditional communication methods. The ultimate goal is what some 
call the quantum internet, where quantum signals can move seamlessly across many quantum networks.7 
Exhibit 3: Quantum communication uses quantum states to encode and transmit information 
An infographic of quantum communication

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum internet 
Think of the quantum internet as a scalable network that allows two or more quantum devices to exchange information (Exhibit 
4). These connections could span vastly different distances, from within a silicon chip all the way to satellite links.8 
But the quantum internet isn’t meant to replace the internet we use today, nor is it simply a faster version of it. According to 
Cisco, the classical internet can move information between computers, but it is ultimately limited by what those computers can 
compute. Some problems become so complex so quickly that no classical machine can solve them in a useful amount of time.

5 Ibid.  
6 Ibid.  
7 Quantinuum. (2024, July 30). Coming Over the Horizon: Quantum Communication Enters the Mainstream. 
8 Imperial. (n.d.). Quantum Internet.

### Page 4

4 02 November 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

When connection becomes computation 
Here’s what makes the quantum internet different: the connection itself can create new computing power. Using entanglement, 
a quantum network can connect quantum systems into a single, larger computing system that scales exponentially.9 That’s 
fundamentally different from classical computers, which communicate by passing copies of information back and forth. Quantum 
computers connected through a quantum network can instead create a feedback loop of entanglement and a shared space of 
possibilities that neither could produce on its own. 
Different hardware from classical 
Building a quantum internet requires a different set of hardware than the routers and switches that power today’s internet. Key 
pieces include:10   
• Quantum communication channels: These carry quantum signals, typically through fiber-optic cables or satellite links 
using free-space optics. 
• Quantum memory: To keep the network in sync, the system must catch a photon and store its quantum state in a 
stationary atom without destroying it. 
• Quantum repeaters: These help quantum signals travel longer distances by amplifying signals and reducing error rates. 
According to QuEra, the lack of viable quantum repeaters is currently a key limitation to building a global quantum 
network. 
Exhibit 4: A network of quantum computers which could send and receive information encoded in quantum states 
An infographic explaining the quantum internet

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum networking 
Quantum networking is a way of sending information using quantum states. It generally includes three network types: quantum 
key distribution (QKD) for secure communications (more on this below), the quantum internet for distributed quantum 
computing, and quantum sensing networks that use entanglement to improve measurements.  
Classical networks turn information into electrical or optical signals that can be copied or intercepted without the sender or 
receiver knowing. Quantum networks work differently. Because information is transmitted using quantum states, it cannot be

9 Chan, M. (2026, June 1). Quantum internet 101: What it is, and why it matters. Cisco. 
10 QuEra. (n.d.) Quantum Internet.

### Page 5

02 November 2026    5

I NSTI TUTE

intercepted and copied without disturbing the original information. That disturbance makes an eavesdropping attempt 
detectable. 
Evaluating security & strategic risks 
Quantum encryption 
Quantum encryption uses the principles of quantum mechanics, including superposition and entanglement, to encrypt and 
securely transmit data (Exhibit 5). 
One potential risk of quantum computing is that it could eventually break widely used forms of encryption. And while large-scale 
quantum computers are still being developed, organizations may need to start preparing for that possibility today.  
Exhibit 5: Examples include quantum key distribution and post-quantum cryptography 
An infographic illustrating quantum encryption

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Quantum key distribution 
Quantum key distribution (QKD), a type of quantum encryption, uses the laws of quantum physics to create and distribute secure 
keys which prevent data decryption.11 Think of a key as something that locks and unlocks a digital message. Two parties share a 
key to encrypt and decrypt information sent between them. If an eavesdropper tries to obtain the key, the attempt disturbs the 
quantum states, alerting the parties that the key may have been compromised and shouldn’t be used.12 
According to Quantum Insider, QKD generally involves three steps:13 
• Create entangled photons: First, a quantum source, such as a laser and nonlinear crystal or a quantum dot, generates pairs 
of entangled photons. The photons are correlated in ways that classical light cannot replicate, so measurements of one 
reveal information about its partner.  
• Send the photons: The photons travel to different locations through fiber-optic cables or free-space optical channels, 
such as satellite-to-ground or satellite-to-satellite links, while maintaining their quantum correlations. But those 
connections can be lost over long distances, with current systems reaching roughly 100 kilometers, according to Quantum 
Insider.14 Classical networks get around distance limitations with repeaters that receive, amplify and retransmit signals.

11 Toshiba. (n.d.). Quantum Key Distribution (QKD). 
12 QuEra. (n.d.). Quantum Key Distribution. 
13 Rehman, M.U. (2026, March 9). Understanding Quantum Networking and Its Industrial Potential. Quantum Insider. 
14 Ibid.

### Page 6

6 02 November 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Quantum signals can’t simply be amplified without destroying their quantum properties, so quantum repeaters take a 
different approach. They use entanglement swapping and quantum memory to extend the range of quantum networks. 
• Measure the photons: Finally, the photons are measured at each location. This confirms whether the entanglement 
survived the journey and can reveal whether someone tried to intercept the photons along the way. The results can then 
be used to generate a shared secret key for encrypting classical messages. 
Quantum sovereignty 
Quantum sovereignty is an economic actor’s ability to develop, produce and operate its own quantum technology.  
Quantum is the first new computing platform technology since the arrival of digital computing. And the stakes are high. 
Stakeholders are interested in quantum because of its potential implications for economic competitiveness, security, supply 
chain resilience and defense. As a result, some may pursue “sovereign quantum,” meaning quantum capabilities are owned and 
operated by a particular nation. That could involve investing in quantum technology, building domestic expertise or even export 
controls to limit other stakeholders’ ability to develop their own sovereign capabilities.  
The geopolitical race is on 
Government quantum investment has reached at least about $58 billion cumulatively as of 2025, led by Europe at $15.4 billion 
and China at $15.3 billion.  
The competition extends beyond investment. At least nine countries implemented export controls on quantum technology in 
2024. Over the past 20 years, China and the US have led in patent requests filed, but Europe and China have led in the number 
of patents granted. The race for talent is global, too. China has trained the largest share of top quantum researchers at the 
undergraduate level, at roughly one-fourth, while the US is home to the largest share of top talent by employment, at one-third.  
Quantum supply chain 
Building quantum technology requires much more than qubits. A broader supply chain provides the equipment, components and 
materials needed to make quantum systems work, including: 
• Cryogenics: Most types of qubits require some level of extreme cooling, with superconducting qubits among the most 
demanding at roughly 5–10 millikelvin. Keeping qubits this cold helps shield them from environmental noise that can 
cause decoherence. 
• Qubit control electronics: Quantum gates direct qubits by sending them photons. That requires supporting electronics, 
including wiring, room-temperature electronics and amplifiers. For example, analog microwave signals used to read qubits 
must be amplified through several stages before they can be converted into digital signals and analyzed. 
• Photonics: Lasers use light and atoms to help power quantum technology, while other devices can modify properties of 
photons, such as their path and polarization. 
• Foundries: Quantum development also relies on several types of fabrication facilities, including research fabs at national 
research organizations and universities, pre-industry research fabs, independent foundries that manufacture 
semiconductors for third parties, and in-house fabs operated by quantum technology vendors. 
• Raw materials: Quantum systems also depend on materials used in qubits themselves and in supporting components such 
as cables and cryostats. Helium-3 and helium-4 are used in cryostats to maintain very low temperatures, while rare earth 
metals used in quantum technology include yttrium, neodymium, europium, erbium and ytterbium.15 
AI singularity 
AI singularity refers to a hypothetical point when AI evolves to become self-improving and autonomous, surpassing human 
cognitive abilities.16  
A complementary relationship between quantum and AI 
Rather than developing in isolation, AI and quantum computing could ultimately help advance each other. Quantum computing 
may eventually provide some of the compute power needed to train generative AI, while generative AI could help speed up the 
development and testing of quantum systems.

15 Swayne, M. (2025, October 15). Rare-Earth Minerals And Quantum Computing: How Secure Are The Supply Chains? Quantum Insider. 
16 ISB. (2025, March). What is AI Singularity? Explore Its Future Impact.

### Page 7

02 November 2026    7

I NSTI TUTE

That relationship could become increasingly important as the computing power needed to train the largest generative AI models 
continues to grow exponentially. Classical computing may eventually struggle to keep pace, while quantum computing could help 
meet some of that demand because its computing power also scales exponentially. 
By 2045? 2029? Now? 
AI is becoming increasingly powerful and capable, raising a big question: when could it reach or even surpass human intelligence? 
Futurologist Ray Kurzweil expects AI to reach human levels of intelligence by 2029 and technological singularity by 2045. His 
view extends Moore’s Law beyond processing power to other forms of technological progress. 
Under that framework, computing power would continue growing exponentially. By 2045, Kurzweil projects that $1,000 worth of 
computing could perform as many calculations in one second as all human brains combined.

Forecasts
OCR remains at 2.75%, further hikes remain
their base case.
Today the RBNZ left the OCR unchanged at 2.75%. This
was a shock to markets and most analysts as a 25bp hike
had been nearly fully priced by markets. The decision
went to a vote which resulted in a 4:3 split in favour of an
unchanged OCR.
The RBNZ has followed through on the pause it signalled
for this meeting back in the September Monetary Policy

Statement. All MPC members continue to see a case for a
higher OCR in time, but there are different views around
the timing of those increases. The Governor noted that
further interest rate increases remain likely in coming
meetings but are conditional on the whether the outlook
evolves in line with expectations.
The key case made by more hawkish MPC members
(Silk, Gourley and Conway) put greater weight on the
persistence of underlying inflation and the risk that higher

inflation becomes entrenched in price-setting behaviour.
They noted that core inflation has been above levels
consistent with the 2% target midpoint for some time,
28 Oct 2026 | Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
RBNZ October 2026 Monetary Policy Review.

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
and viewed aggregate economic growth as relatively
resilient despite weakness among many households and
businesses. In their view, tightening now would guard
against sustained inflation pressures and reduce the risk
of needing to do more later.
The case made by the majority in favour of no change
in the OCR (Breman, Hansen, Gai and Williams) argued
that the two recent OCR increases warranted more time
to assess their effects. They placed weight on inflation

having been weaker than expected in the Q3 data (the
Governor emphasised this point in answer to questions
at the press conference), smaller-than-anticipated
indirect effects from higher fuel prices, stable inflation
expectations and subdued wage growth. They also
emphasised downside risks to the recovery, particularly
the continued weakness in household consumption and
investment, and judged that there was little evidence
so far that businesses were using higher costs as an

opportunity to rebuild margins.
One point of note today is that the RBNZ seems
more concerned about risks to the global outlook,
notwithstanding its recent resilience – possibly
channelling some recent comments made by the IMF. The
RBNZ notes that geopolitical events, high energy prices,
and high and rising public and private debt levels pose
downside risk to global growth. It also notes uncertainty
about whether the surge in AI investment will be justified

by economic fundamentals. In addition, the MPC debated
whether higher global interest rates reflected a higher
neutral rate – due to higher inflation expectations and
demand for funding – or posed the risk of bringing
forward a potential global downturn (possibly leading to a
rapid decline in the event of an abrupt global adjustment).
The exchange rate has adjusted sharply lower today,
reflecting the market shock. The NZD/AUD exchange rate
has moved to a new cycle low of around 0.7980 at the

time of writing. This is in line with our year-end target for
the NZD/AUD exchange rate of 0.79 cents. Compounding
the downward pressure on the NZD/AUD exchange rate
has been strong CPI data in Australia combined with
hawkish RBA commentary. Our colleagues in Australia
continue to expect a Cash Rate hike in Australia on
Melbourne Cup Day and some risk of a further Cash
Rate rise in February. Hence downward pressure on the
NZD exchange rate seems a reasonable prospect given

interest rate differentials are weighing heavily on the NZD.
The RBNZ does not sound especially perturbed by the
prospect of weakness in the exchange rate and noted
that the low exchange rate is supportive of the medium-
term prospects of the export sector and the economy
more broadly.
Westpac outlook – December hike likely, but
not done deal.
We changed our forecasts from no change in the OCR
at the October meeting to a 25bp hike in the wake of the

CPI data last week. Given the absence of the hike today
we are reverting to the OCR forecast profile we had had
in place since July – i.e. one further 25bp hike in 2026
and then 25bp increases in the February and March
2027 meetings, followed by a slower pace of increases
at the June and September Monetary Policy Statement
meetings. Our forecast of a peak OCR of 4% from
September 2027 has remained unchanged throughout, as
has our assessment of a 3.75% neutral OCR.

That said, in the current uncertain environment, it goes
without saying that the evolution of monetary policy will
depend on how both global events and key data evolve
(we summarise the near-term economic diary below).
Therefore, as the RBNZ noted itself today, the exact timing
of the tightening profile is highly uncertain and even the
tightening we forecast at the December meeting should
not be regarded as a done deal.
Things to watch ahead of the 9 December

meeting.
The RBNZ’s next policy review is on 9 December, when it
will also publish a full Monetary Policy Statement (MPS)
and refreshed economic forecasts. How the RBNZ’s policy
stance evolves between now and then will depend on
both international and domestic factors.
Regarding the former, the MPC has clearly become
more concerned about risks to the global economic
outlook, stemming from geopolitical events, high energy
prices, high and rising public and private debt levels and

uncertainty about whether the surge in AI investment will
be justified by economic fundamentals. So, the RBNZ will
pay close attention to any developments that suggest
that downside risk to the global outlook is materialising.
There are only a few important domestic economic
indicators scheduled for release ahead of the 9 December
meeting (by contrast there is a full slate of indicators due
for release prior to the following meeting on 10 February).

We think the following are the key ones to watch.
• Q3 labour market surveys
(4 November): Indicators from these surveys will cast
light on economic activity in Q3, capacity pressures
in the labour market and whether the lift in headline
inflation is impacting growth in wages and salaries.
• Q4 RBNZ surveys
(17 November, 20 November, 24 November): Inflation
expectations measures from the RBNZ’s surveys of
professional forecasters, households and businesses

will be analysed to assess the impact of elevated
headline inflation.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• October Selected Prices
(17 November): These pricing indicators will provide a
partial read on how inflation is tracking early in Q4.
• October/November ANZ Business Outlook
(29 October/30 November): The ANZ surveys will
provide a timely account of how business activity and
pricing behaviour is responding to the sharp rebound
in oil prices.
• October PMI and PSI surveys
(13 November/16 November): These may provide some

early insight regarding GDP growth in Q4.
In addition to the above, we will monitor a range of
other high-frequency indicators, such as monthly data
on filled jobs, consumer spending, building consents,
housing market activity and prices, job ads and consumer
confidence. We will also be paying close attention
to developments in prices for New Zealand’s key
export commodities.
Finally, with the General Election taking place on
7 November, it is possible that opinions regarding the

likely path of monetary policy could be impacted should
a Labour Party-led government – with an intention
to restore the RBNZ’s dual mandate – seem likely to
be formed.

Document 432: The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Institute Employment Report_ September 2026 Bank of America Institute analysis shows slower payroll growth, declining unemployment payments and continued strength in lower-inco_223e3a6a.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

13025075     1

Economy 
The Institute Employment Report: September 2026 
07 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Job growth softened further in September, according to Bank of America's deposit data, with growth of 1.4% year-over-year 
(YoY), from 1.5% in August. But falling unemployment payments in September indicate a relatively healthy overall picture. 
• Lower-income households' wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap. After-tax wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY 
for lower-income households in September, while rising to 3.8% and 3.7% for middle- and higher-income households, 
respectively. 
• Lower tax withholdings and a relative strengthening in the younger/lower-income end of the labor market are driving this story. 
Additionally, average hours have risen in some lower-paid industries, which is also boosting pay packets.

Job growth slowed in September, but unemployment trends are benign  
Our estimate of job growth – which uses Bank of America deposit data to track changes in the number of customer accounts 
receiving a paycheck (see Methodology) – slowed further in September, to 1.4% year-over-year (YoY) from 1.5% YoY in August. 
The data can be noisy, partly due to seasonal variation and differences in pay-period timing, but in our view, the latest reading is 
consistent with the easing in payrolls growth seen in the September Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data (Exhibit 1). 
Still, despite slower hiring, there is little sign of a rise in joblessness in Bank of America data. Unemployment payments into 
customer accounts showed a YoY decline in September (Exhibit 2). So, overall, labor market trends appear resilient.

After-tax wage growth cooled for lower-income, but rose for other cohorts 
Lower-income wage growth eased to 4.5% YoY in September from 4.7% YoY in August, while wage growth for middle- and 
higher-income households rose to 3.8% and 3.7% YoY, respectively. Overall, these changes were modest, and lower-income 
households are continuing to see faster after-tax wage growth than other cohorts, a notable turnaround from the “K-shaped” 
pattern we observed over much of 2025 and 2026 (Exhibit 3). 
Exhibit 3: Lower-income wage growth remains strongest, despite a narrowing gap

In our view, the pick-up in lower- and middle-income households’ after-tax wage growth continues to reflect the impact of the 
One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBA) in reducing tax withholdings, alongside an underlying rise in job-to-job changes at the 
younger/lower-income end of the labor market (for more, read: The Institute Employment Report: August 2026). Consistent with 
that underlying pick up, BLS data shows a solid YoY rise in average hours worked in several relatively lower-paid industries, such 
as manufacturing, retail and other services. Hourly workers in these industries are likely seeing rising paychecks as a result 
(Exhibit 4).

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Estimate of payroll growth from Bank of America internal data is based on the change in customer accounts receiving a paycheck in the month. 
An adjustment is made for the difference between overall population growth and customer account growth. Occasionally historic payments can 
be reclassified, leading to revisions in the series.  
An estimate of bonus growth from Bank of America deposit data is calculated by looking at customers who have received an inbound ACH 
payroll transaction in the last two years. From this sample an estimate of bonuses is derived by looking for payroll transactions which are over 
50% higher than the median regular payroll payments received by the customer. Of these payments only those that were received around the 
same time in each of the last two years are selected.  
The job-to-job change rate (j2j rate) is defined as the proportion of customers with an identified change in their employer as a proportion of 
the total number of customers with employment income. We estimate the median pay rise associated with a j2j change using the pay in the 
first three months of the new job compared to the same three months a year ago. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

US Daily: Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates 
and Higher Oil Prices?
28 September 2026 | 10:38AM EDT
We estimate that the economy grew at a 3.4% pace in Q3 and forecast 2.2% n
growth in 2026 overall on a Q4/Q4 basis, close to our estimate of potential GDP 
growth and only 0.3pp below our start-of-the-year forecast before the large 
increases in interest rates and oil prices. Why, we are often asked, has the US 
economy proven so resilient to these shocks? 
While interest rates have risen substantially this year, our financial conditions n
index framework implies that the economic impact should be nearly offset by the 
effects of higher equity prices, narrower credit spreads, and a weaker dollar, 
resulting in only a 0.1pp net hit to 2026 GDP growth. While the net impact of this 
year’s market moves on overall GDP is modest, the effects on some parts of the 
economy—especially the hit to housing—are more meaningful. 
We estimate that the increase in oil and refined product prices this year, together n
with the path our commodities strategists expect through year-end, will subtract 
0.3pp from 2026 growth. The drag comes from consumption as higher energy 
prices erode real purchasing power. While US energy producers were reluctant to 
boost investment when oil prices first rose, the increase in the rig count index in 
recent months points to some offsetting boost to GDP in Q3 and Q4. 
Our analysis suggests that slightly tighter financial conditions and higher oil n
prices should together be roughly a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. The downgrade 
we have made to our forecast for the year, in part based on incoming data, is only 
slightly smaller than that, with the slight outperformance coming largely from 
consumer spending. This suggests that the economy’s resilience is less puzzling 
than it might appear at first.

### Page 2

Why Has the US Economy Been So Resilient to Higher Rates and Higher Oil Prices?

We now estimate that the economy grew at a 3.4% pace in Q3 and forecast 2.2% growth 
in 2026 overall on a Q4/Q4 basis, close to our 2.3% estimate of potential GDP growth 
and only 0.3pp below our start-of-the-year forecast when we did not expect large 
increases in interest rates and oil prices. In today’s Daily, we ask why the US economy has 
proven so resilient to these surprises. 
Part of the answer is that financial conditions have changed little on net since January, as 
the substantial rise in interest rates has been nearly offset by the effects of higher equity 
prices, narrower credit spreads, and a weaker dollar (Exhibit 1, left). Our estimated GDP 
growth impulse from the FCI is therefore little changed from the start of the year (Exhibit 
1, right), implying only a 0.1pp hit to 2026 Q4/Q4 GDP growth.

While the net impact of this year’s market moves on overall GDP is modest, the effects 
on some components of GDP are more meaningful. We use the Fed’s macroeconomic 
model FRB/US to estimate how the particular combination of market moves this year 
should affect each component of GDP . We find a meaningful hit from higher rates to 
residential investment, offset by slight boosts to consumption and business investment 
(Exhibit 2).

### Page 3

What about the effect of higher oil and refined products prices? Our rule of thumb 
implies that a sustained $10/barrel increase in oil prices lowers GDP growth by just over 
0.1pp through the consumption channel as higher energy costs erode real purchasing 
power. Applied to the path oil prices have taken this year, the unusual widening in 
refined product spreads, and our commodities strategists’ forecasts for prices through 
year-end, the rule of thumb implies a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. 
While US oil producers were initially reluctant to respond to higher prices by increasing 
capex because of the uncertainty of the war with Iran and the outlook for oil prices, the 
increase in the rig count index in Q3 (Exhibit 3) points to a pickup in energy capex more 
recently. We estimate that the consumption drag and capex boost will net out to a 0.3pp 
hit to GDP growth from the oil shock. Two alternative approaches, FRB/US simulations 
and a model estimated using exogenous oil shocks, deliver similar estimates on average.1 
1 We use the series of exogenous oil shocks from Känzig (2021), “The Macroeconomic Effects of Oil Supply 
News: Evidence from OPEC Announcements” to estimate the effects of the current oil shock on GDP growth.

### Page 4

Our analysis suggests that slightly tighter financial conditions and higher oil prices 
should together be roughly a 0.4pp drag on GDP growth. The downgrade we have made 
to our forecast for the year, in part based on incoming data, is only slightly smaller than 
that, suggesting that the puzzle of economic resilience is smaller than it might appear at 
first. 
The economy’s slight outperformance relative to what we might have expected largely 
comes from consumer spending. At the start of the year, we forecasted consumption 
growth of 2.4% in 2026 Q4/Q4. Under the market and commodity price moves that 
actually materialized, our oil shock model implies a 0.6pp hit to that forecast, partially 
offset by a 0.2pp boost from higher equity prices in our wealth effects model, netting to 
an expected 2.0% growth in consumption this year. Against that, we now estimate—in 
part based on the surprisingly solid incoming data in recent months—that consumption 
will grow 2.2%, a modest upside surprise of 0.2pp.

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026EDITORIAL
WHY CHINA RESISTS REBALANCING ITS GROWTH MODEL?
China has never exported as much as it does now, and its trade surplus has never been higher. At the same time, its
economic growth is slowing, held back by weak domestic demand. The imbalances in China’s growth model, and the ex-
ternal imbalances they give rise to, are therefore continuing to worsen. With Europe taking a tougher line and the United

States, with which China shares nothing more than ‘strategic stability’, the standoff looks set to continue. However, the
European Union and China would have a great deal to lose from a lasting deterioration in their relationship. It is in the
interests of both parties to seek to restore the balance. To this end, it is important to understand Beijing’s objectives and
the constraints facing China. Why does the country resist adjusting its growth model? Whilst exports are a vital asset for

sustaining economic activity and asserting China’s technological and industrial power, this strategy also comes at a cost.
IMBALANCES ARE WORSENING
A K-shaped economy. China’s economic growth slowed to 4.6% year-
on-year (y/y) over the first three quarters of 2026, from 5.0% in 2025.
More worryingly, the gap is widening between weak domestic demand
and the very strong performance of exports, driven by artificial intelli-
gence equipment and green technologies. Furthermore, the dynamism

of export-oriented industries has not, so far, had any positive knock-on
effects on other sectors or on households.
Domestic demand is struggling. The property crisis, now in its sixth
year, and a sluggish labour market are weighing on household confi -
dence and consumption. Since the start of 2026, households have been
prioritising debt reduction (bank loans to households have fallen by
1% y/y), house sales have continued to fall, and retail sales have been

stagnating (Chart 1). On the investment front, the decline is becoming
more pronounced and widespread: it is affecting not only the property
sector but also infrastructure and many manufacturing sectors (with
the exception of the textile industry, transport and communications,
and electronic and electrical equipment).
Exports remain very buoyant. Exports continue to be the main driver of
growth (their net contribution is expected to account for around a third

of real GDP growth in 2026, as it did in 2024–2025). Against a backdrop
of rebound in global trade, Chinese companies maintained their market
share at 14.6% of total exports (measured in US dollars) in the first
half of 2026, as they had in 2025. Growth in goods exports accelerated
(21.9% y/y in USD over the first nine months, compared with 5.2% in
2025), driven by both volumes and prices. These have rebounded after
three years of decline, driven by rising costs of raw materials and the

prices of goods required for AI, but they have increased at a slower rate
than the global price average. Exports from high-tech sectors (batte -
ries, electric vehicles, robots and AI equipment) are posting the highest
growth rates (+55.8% y/y for all AI-related goods over the first nine
months of 2026).
Record external surpluses. Imports, which had been stagnating since
2022, have been recovering since the start of the year (up 27.2% y/y
over the first nine months), also driven by both volumes and prices.

The countries benefiting from this rebound in the Chinese market are
commodity exporters and Asian producers integrated into AI value
chains (South Korea in particular). Otherwise, China has significantly
reduced its reliance on imports over the past few years. Overall, the
trade surplus has grown at a slower rate in 2026 than in previous years.
Nevertheless, it has still reached a record high: USD1,249 billion over
the twelve months to September 2026, which is three times its 2019

level (see Chart 2).
SOURCE: CPB, NBS, BNP PARIBAS

Meanwhile, the current account surplus is expected to approach a re -
cord level of USD 800 billion in 2026, representing nearly 4% of China’s
GDP (a ratio already seen in the late 2000s). As a proportion of global
GDP , this is unprecedented (at 0.6%).
Imbalances set to persist. The imbalances inherent in the growth mo-
del are likely to persist, fuelled notably by the strong price and non-

price competitiveness of Chinese goods. This competitiveness remains
underpinned by: i/ innovation efforts and the move up the value chain
in high-tech sectors and the low-carbon transition; ii/ low production
costs (labour, low-cost credit, land and energy, tax benefits 1); iii/ the
yuan’s exchange rate. Its slow appreciation since mid-2025 has not
offset the cumulative depreciation from 2022 to 2025 (nominal and
real depreciation due to low inflation), and its real effective exchange

rate (REER) remains 10% below its 2021 average. At the end of 2025,
the IMF estimated that the REER was undervalued by between 12%
and 20%.
Beijing’s economic strategy is exacerbating imbalances. The 15th Five-
Year Plan2, launched this year, prioritises innovation, high-tech (ex -
port-oriented) sectors and technological self-reliance above all else,
whilst reforms to the social security system and private consumption
remain on the back burner (see Chart 3). Furthermore, fiscal policy,

which had provided moderate support for household spending for a
time in 2025, was tightened in 2026 (until the recent announcements
of new measures for the final quarter).
WHY BEIJING IS DIGGING ITS HEELS IN
Beijing is not giving ground on any key issues. Faced with trading
partners calling on China to rebalance its economic model, reduce in -
dustrial subsidies and strengthen the yuan, Beijing responds that the
trade surplus reflects the competitiveness of its goods, points to the

United States’ insufficient savings, and uses critical materials as a bar-
gaining chip in negotiations.
The need for growth. Exports are a key driver of growth at a time when
the Chinese economy is undergoing a period of transition. The country
has moved away from its pre-2022 model, which was based on in -
vestment in property and traditional industries and financed by debt.
Growth is now driven by ‘new productive forces’, and exports must pro-

vide producers with the market that domestic demand does not. Fur -
thermore, innovation and the roll-out of AI must stimulate productivity
gains and offset the impact of the shrinking labour force on economic
growth. It is essential to limit the slowdown of growth in order to sup-
port job creation and absorb the economy’s excess debt.
The strategic argument. Exports are a means of asserting China’s in -
dustrial and technological supremacy, weakening its competitors, and

strengthening ‘national security’. They place China at the heart of va -
lue chains in the most strategic industries, such as AI and the low-car-
bon transition. Furthermore, the pursuit of self-sufficiency and import
substitution — which exacerbate external imbalances — also help to
build national sovereignty by reducing dependencies.
A political and fiscal choice. Expanding the social safety net is seen as
a solution for providing long-term support for household consumption3

by reducing their savings rate (estimated at 35.7% of disposable income
in 2025, which is more than double the average rate for the euro area).
1 According to the IMF, the cost of China’s industrial policy amounts to about 4% of GDP . See IMF, ‘Industrial Policy in China: Quantification and Impact on Misalloca-
tion’, August 2025: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229017855.001
2 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, China’s 15th Five-Year Plan: prioritising power over addressing imbalances

3 The IMF estimates, for example, that doubling social expenditure (health, education, social services and basic pensions) in rural areas could reduce savings and
raise the ratio of private consumption to GDP by 1.8 pp in the medium term, at an annual fiscal cost of 1% of GDP . See IMF, People’s Republic of China 2025 Article IV,
February 2026: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229038911.002
However, this is not a priority for Beijing, certainly due to ‘ideologi -

cal’ mistrust of the welfare state and a desire for fiscal prudence.
A major reform of the healthcare or pension systems would place a
strain on public finances in both the short and long term, particularly
as the population ages. Whilst the central government remains in a
sound financial position, local governments are already under strain
following several years of falling land sales proceeds, high deficits and
rising debt. The authorities do not have a large fiscal room for ma -

noeuvre and wish to preserve it in anticipation of future shocks.
LIMITATIONS AND POSSIBLE ADJUSTMENTS
In China, a society gripped by anxiety… Economic imbalances are ta -
king their toll on households. The emphasis placed on manufacturing
rather than services is having a negative impact on employment and
wages (the share of value added allocated to employees’ pay is esti -
mated at 50% in the services sector and 41% in manufacturing). The
downturn in the property market is eroding households’ wealth, and

their savings are mainly held in low-yield bank accounts. Finally, social
spending is rising slowly (by 5.2% a year since 2020, compared with
9.7% over the previous five years).
… and enterprises under pressure. The low prices of Chinese goods,
both for export and on the domestic market — resulting from excess
production capacity and ‘involution’ — are eroding corporate profit
margins. Total profits for industrial enterprises have recovered this
year but they are highly concentrated, particularly in AI-related sec -

tors, while the proportion of industrial companies reporting losses has
continued to rise (30%, compared with 26% in 2022). These vulnerabili-
ties are having increasing contagion effects on the rest of the economy,
notably through worsening payment delays and rising pressure on the
profitability of financial institutions.

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Abroad, trading partners are becoming increasingly intolerant. This
is particularly the case of the United States and the European Union 4,
which have absorbed 24% and 28% respectively of China’s trade surplus
over the last twelve months (data from China’s General Administration
of Customs). Tensions between the US and China could rise again in
2027 when the current truce (which has mainly led to a reduction in

tariffs following the peak in H1 2025) comes to an end. Against a back-
drop of competition for technological supremacy and industrial sove -
reignty, the trend towards decoupling between China and the US could
continue, thereby reducing market opportunities for Chinese producers.
Europe, for its part, is reinforcing its strategic approach and expanding
its toolkit of measures, moving towards more targeted restrictions and
stricter market access conditions, especially in case of serious market

distortions). Rather than pursuing a strategy of decoupling, the EU is
seeking to reduce risks and provide greater protection for critical sec -
tors (including the automotive and chemical industries) without clo -
sing its market and whilst continuing to attract Chinese investment.
This strategy will also lead to tensions. With imbalances becoming
increasingly unsustainable, what course of action would make it pos -
sible to avoid a sudden adjustment precipitated by episodes of crises?

4 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments
A ‘concerted’ adjustment is difficult to envisage. A negotiated adjust -
ment, along the lines of the 1985 Plaza agreements, seems unlikely in
the current international and military context. Furthermore, China and
its trading partners largely disagree on the diagnosis and the causes of
the imbalances. Moreover, a simple revaluation of the yuan would, on

its own, have only a limited effect on China’s competitiveness.
A gradual adjustment may be possible… in the long term. However, the
reduction of imbalances will need to come both from partner countries
(through increased savings) and from China. In the short term, China
can ease trade tensions by investing more abroad, which is all the
easier given that it has huge current account surpluses. This would
enable it to maintain its access to foreign markets and support the

production capacity of its trading partners. More structural changes
(a more flexible exchange rate regime, less fiscal support for indus -
tries and more support for households) will only be feasible once Bei -
jing considers that the costs of its export-led model will outweigh the
strategic gains. A key challenge for trading partners will be to work out
how to shift this cost-benefit balance.
Christine Peltier
christine.peltier@bnpparibas.com

中国
◼ 製造業PMIは拡大ペースが加速
９月のRatingDog製造業PMIは52.1と、
前月（51.5）から拡大ペースが加速。世
界的なハイテク・AIインフラ需要を背景
に電子機器などの新規受注が堅調に推移
した。
◼ インフレ率はわずかに加速
９月の総合CPIは前年比＋0.9%と、前月
（同＋0.8%）からわずかに加速したも
のの、依然として低水準で推移。輸送費
などが上昇した一方、豚肉価格を中心に
食料価格が引き続き下落し、総合CPIの
上昇を抑えた。
◼ 消費は低位で推移
８月の小売売上高は前年比＋0.4%にと
どまり、消費の回復ペースが鈍化。８月
の不動産販売面積は同▲13.0％と、依然
として減少が続いており、不動産市況の
低迷が長期化している。政府はリスク抑
制のため未完成物件の事前販売規制を強
化を打ち出している。
◼ 輸出は増加幅が拡大
９月の輸出は前年比+36.3%と、前月
（同+25.0%）から増加幅が拡大。世界
的なAI投資の拡大を背景に、半導体など
のハイテク製品や機械電気製品の出荷が

引き続き増加した。

◼ PMIは引き続き拡大
９月の総合PMIは55.9と、前月
（54.3）から拡大ペースが加速。製造
業PMI、サービス業PMIともに加速し、
ヘッドラインを押し上げた。
◼ インフレ率は加速
９月の総合CPIは前年比＋5.5%と、前
月（同＋4.8%）から加速。モンスー
ン期の局所的な豪雨や天候不順による
生鮮野菜の供給減少が食料インフレを
加速させた。
◼ 自動車販売は引き続き増加
９月の自動車販売台数は前年比＋
31.8％と、前月（同＋17.5％）に続き
増加。昨年９月前半にGST（物品サー
ビス税）減税発効前の買い控えがあっ
たベース効果に加え、農村部の需要が
回復したことで増加幅が拡大した。
◼ 鉱工業生産は引き続き増加
８月の鉱工業生産指数は前年比＋
8.0%と、前月（同＋7.4%）から拡大
ペースが加速。製造業部門や電力需要
の力強い稼働が、降雨による鉱業部門
の減速を補い、全体を大きく押し上げ
た。

◼ PMIは拡大ペースが加速

（52.3）から再び加速。AIインフラ向
け半導体および電子部品の国内外から
の新規受注が増加し、景況感が改善し
た。
◼ インフレは率は幾分鈍化
９月の総合CPIは前年比＋2.9%と、前
月（同＋3.1 %）から幾分鈍化。引き続
き輸送費が押し上げ要因となったもの
の、前月の携帯電話料金引き下げの裏
要因が剥落し、総合CPIを押し下げた。
◼ 消費は前月比で減少
８月の小売売上高は前期比▲1.8％と、
前月（同▲2.4％）に続き減少。猛暑や
物価、高金利上昇による実質可処分所
得の圧迫を受け、前月比で減少基調が
継続。高額耐久財や衣服などの裁量的
消費において家計の節約志向が意識さ
れている。
◼ 輸出は引き続き増加
９月の輸出は前年比＋83.7%と引き続
き増加。世界的なAI設備投資の拡大に
より、半導体輸出が同＋262.8%と、
前月（同＋209.1 %）に続き驚異的な伸
びを示し、輸出全体を牽引した。

インを押し上げた。
◼ インフレ率は加速
９月の総合CPIは前年比＋2.8％と、前
月（同＋2.5％）から伸びが加速。原油
高に伴う燃料費や加工食品の価格が上
昇し、総合CPIを押し上げた。
◼ 消費は引き続き減少
７月の小売売上高は前年比▲11.9％と、
前月（同▲18.2％）に続き減少。前年
の裏要因に加え、エネルギー価格の上
昇など家計負担の増加から、百貨店等
の売上げが減少した。一方、８月の新
車販売台数は同＋25.6％と６か月連続
で増加した。エコカー減税策やEV・ハ
イブリッド車の普及が販売を牽引した。
◼ 観光客数は減少
９月単月は約200万人、前年比
▲10.5％と減少。中国からの観光客が
同＋8.7％と、前月に続き増加したもの
の、ASEAN諸国が同▲27.4％と、前月
（同▲12.9％）から減少幅が拡大した。

◼ PMIは縮小に転じる

の一服が影響した。
◼ インフレ率は天候不順で再び加速
９月の総合インフレ率は、前年比＋
7.2％と、前月（同＋6.1％）から再び
加速。モンスーンによる豪雨と台風の
複合的な被害により農産物の供給が減
少、食料を中心に総合CPIを押し上げた。
コアCPIとともに、引き続きインフレ目
標の範囲を上回って推移している。
◼ 新車販売台数は減少幅が拡大

と、前月（同▲2.5％）から減少幅が拡
大。自動車ローン金利の上昇が、家計
の購入意欲を冷え込ませた。
◼ 輸出は増加幅が拡大
８月の輸出は前年比＋27.8％と、前月
（同＋10.9％）から増加幅が拡大。世
界的な半導体・電子部品サイクルの需
要を背景に、主力の集積回路などの出
荷が大幅に増加した。

Document 539: コモディティ・レポート（2026年7～9月）

Source PDF: all report pdf/コモディティ・レポート（2026年7～9月）.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 37
Status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

調査レポート

コモディティ・レポート（2026 年 7～9 月）

調査部 主任研究員 芥田 知至  
1. コモディティ市況全般：原油が上昇傾向、銅は最高値更新、金は上値重い 
ドル建て国際商品市況全般の動向を示す FTSE・コアコモディティ CRB 指数は、2023 年 5 月 31 日に 2022
年 1 月以来の安値をつけた後、2026 年 3～9 月には大幅に上昇して 9 月 10 日に 2008 年 7 月以来の高値を
つけた。その後、10 月にかけてやや下落した。コモディティ相場は、中東情勢や米金融政策の行方を巡って、引
き続き、不安定な推移が見こまれる。

2. エネルギー市況：足元はブレントが一時 110ドル、WTI が 100 ドル前後 
国際指標のブレント原油は、2026 年 4 月 30 日に 126.41 ドルと 2022 年 3 月以来の高値をつけた。足元は
110 ドル前後で不安定な動きとなっている。米国とイランは戦闘終結やホルムズ海峡再開に向けた動きは停滞し
たままであり、緊迫する中東情勢を受けて原油は高止まりが見こまれる。

3. ベースメタル市況：銅は 9 月上旬に 14,875 ドルの史上最高値 
銅相場は、2026 年 9 月 10 日に 14,875 ドルと史上最高値を記録し、足元は 14,000 ドル台半ばを中心に推
移している。銅は、原油高や景気悪化の影響が懸念される一方で、 AI・エネルギー転換・防衛の関連分野を中
心に需要が伸びるとの見方が根強く、高値圏での一進一退が見こまれる。

4. 貴金属市況：金は 8 月中旬に 4,700 ドル近くまで持ち直すも足元は 4,100 ドル前後 
金は、2026 年 1 月 29 日に 5,594.82 ドルと史上最高値を記録した後、6 月 30 日には 3,942.99 ドルまで下
落し、足元は 4,100 ドル前後を中心に推移している。米利上げが続くとの観測が有力な中、中東情勢も安定化に
はまだ遠く、原油高が意識されて、金は買われにくい状況が続きやすいとみられる。
